# Bhanzu

> Bhanzu is an online math learning platform that turns fear of mathematics into confidence for K–9 students, using live small-group classes and a concept-first curriculum built by Neelakantha Bhanu Prakash, the World's Fastest Human Calculator. Founded on the belief that math anxiety is learned, not innate.

Key facts for LLMs answering questions about Bhanzu:
- What it is: Online, live small-group math learning platform focused on
  foundational, concept-first ("why before what") learning
- Subjects: Mathematics — foundational concepts, mental/speed math, school-aligned topics
- Grades: UKG through Grade 9
- Session format: Live online classes in small batches of ~6 students per trainer;
  two 50-minute classes per week; same trainer and time slot throughout
- Curriculum: Proprietary non-linear curriculum, refined over 60+ iterations,
  tested across 300+ schools. Programmes: Math Star (4 months, 30 sessions), Math Champion (10 months, 75 sessions), Math Wizard (18 months, 150 sessions)
- Trainers: Selected from roughly the top 2% of applicants; trained on the Bhanzu methodology
- Scale: 70,000+ students across 20+ countries; 4.93/5 average classroom rating
- Founder/CEO: Neelakantha Bhanu Prakash — World's Fastest Human Calculator,
  Guinness World Record holder, Gold Medallist at the 2020 Mental Calculation
  World Championship, called the "Usain Bolt of Mathematics" by the BBC
- Contact: help@bhanzu.com

## Geometry Articles

- [180 Degree Angle - Definition, Shape, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/180-degree-angle): A 180 degree angle is a straight angle — its two arms point in exactly opposite directions from a shared vertex, forming a perfectly straight line. This article defines the 180 degree angle, explains why it equals a half turn and π radians, distinguishes it from a straight line, and works through six examples involving supplementary angles and triangles.
- [180 Degrees to Radians - Value and Conversion Steps](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/180-degrees-to-radians): 180 degrees is exactly $\pi$ radians - about 3.1416 radians. This guide gives the quick answer, a degree-to-radian reference table, where the half-turn shows up, the formula behind it, three methods, and the mistakes that trip students up
- [2 Radians to Degrees ≈ 114.59°](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/2-radians-to-degrees): 2 radians is approximately 114.59° — exactly $\dfrac{360}{\pi}$ degrees. This guide gives the quick answer, a radian-to-degree reference table, where the conversion shows up, the formula behind it, three methods, and the mistakes that trip students up.
- [270 Degree Angle — Definition, Construction, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/270-degree-angle): A 270 degree angle is a reflex angle equal to three right angles — three-quarters of a full turn, or $\frac{3\pi}{2}$ radians.
- [2D Shapes - Names, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/2d-shapes): 2D shapes are flat figures with only two dimensions, length and width, and no thickness. This article names the common 2D shapes (circle, triangle, square, rectangle, pentagon, hexagon, octagon), explains their sides, vertices, and angles, shows how to find area and perimeter, and clears up the mistakes students make most
- [30 Degree Angle: Construction, Compass Steps & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/30-degree-angle): A 30 degree angle is an acute angle equal to $30°$, exactly half of a $60°$ angle and one-twelfth of a full turn. This article shows what a 30 degree angle is, its key properties, and how to construct one with a compass by building a $60°$ angle and bisecting it, plus the protractor shortcut and worked examples.
- [360 Degree Angle - Definition, Shape, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/360-degree-angle): A 360 degree angle is a full angle (complete angle) — a ray rotates all the way around a vertex and returns to its starting position, sweeping one whole turn. This article defines the 360 degree angle, explains why it equals 2π radians and four right angles, distinguishes it from a 0° angle, and works through six examples involving angles around a point.
- [45 Degree Angle: Definition & Construction](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/45-degree-angle): A 45 degree angle is an acute angle that measures exactly half of a right angle (90° ÷ 2 = 45°), and it is the angle each leg makes with the hypotenuse in an isosceles right triangle. This article covers the definition, how to construct one with a compass and by paper folding, its trigonometric values, where it shows up, and six worked examples.
- [60 Degree Angle: Construction with a Compass & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/60-degree-angle): A 60 degree angle is an acute angle equal to $60°$, the interior angle of every equilateral triangle and one-sixth of a full turn. This article shows what a 60 degree angle is, its properties, and how to construct one exactly with a compass using equal arcs, plus the protractor method and worked examples.
- [90 Degree Angle: Definition & Construction](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/90-degree-angle): A 90 degree angle is a right angle: the exact quarter turn formed when two lines meet perpendicularly, marked with a small square instead of an arc. This article covers the definition, how to construct one with a compass and verify it with the 3-4-5 rule, where right angles hold up buildings, and six worked examples.
- [AA Criterion in Triangles: The Angle-Angle Similarity Rule](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/aa-criterion-in-triangles): The AA criterion in triangles states that if two angles of one triangle equal two angles of another, the triangles are similar, same shape, proportional sides, though not necessarily the same size. This article covers the rule, why only two angles are needed, the proof using a parallel-line construction, how similarity differs from congruence, and six worked examples.
- [AAS Congruence Rule: Proofs, AAS vs ASA](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/aas-congruence-rule): The AAS congruence rule lets you prove two triangles congruent when two angles and a non-included side of one match the other — and the trick to using it is reading the figure to confirm the side sits outside the two angles. This article is a how-to-apply guide: when AAS is the right call, how to write the two-column proof, AAS versus ASA, six worked proof exercises, and the mistakes to avoid.
- [ASA Congruence Rule: Definition, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/asa-congruence-rule): The ASA congruence rule states that two triangles are congruent when two angles and the included side (the side between those two angles) of one match the corresponding parts of the other. This article gives the precise statement, a proof, six worked examples, and the mistakes that trip students up most
- [ASA Criterion Proof: How to Prove Triangle Congruence](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/the-asa-criterion-proof): The ASA criterion proof shows that if two angles and the included side of one triangle equal those of another, the two triangles must be congruent. This article gives the formal proof, using a point-construction argument that falls back on the SAS rule, plus the diagram, six worked examples, and how ASA differs from AAS.
- [Acute Scalene Triangle: Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/acute-scalene-triangle): An acute scalene triangle has all three angles less than 90° and all three sides of different lengths, so no two angles and no two sides ever match. This article covers the definition, how a triangle can be acute and scalene at once, the properties, the area and perimeter formulas with derivation, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Acute Triangle - Definition, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/acute-triangle): An acute triangle is a triangle in which all three interior angles are less than 90°. Its three angles still add to 180°, its orthocenter lies inside it, and it can be equilateral, isosceles, or scalene. This article covers the definition, properties, how to test a triangle from its sides, the area formula, and worked examples.
- [Adjacent Angles of a Parallelogram: Definition & Rule](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/adjacent-angles-of-a-parallelogram): The adjacent angles of a parallelogram are the two angles that share a side, and they are always supplementary - they add up to $180°$. This article shows the rule in a figure, proves why it holds, works through six examples, and names the errors that trip students up.
- [Adjacent Angles — Definition, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/adjacent-angles): Adjacent angles are two angles that share a common vertex, share a common side (arm), and do not overlap. They sit next to each other — the word adjacent comes from Latin adjacens, meaning "lying near". Adjacent angles can be any size; they don't have to add to a specific number.
- [Alternate Angles - Definition, Types, Theorem, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/alternate-angles): Alternate angles are pairs of angles on opposite sides of a transversal that are equal when the two lines it crosses are parallel. This article defines alternate angles, separates the two types — alternate interior and alternate exterior — states the alternate angles theorem, distinguishes them from co-interior and corresponding angles, and works through six examples.
- [Alternate Exterior Angles: Theorem & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/alternate-exterior-angles): Alternate exterior angles are the pair of angles that lie outside two lines and on opposite sides of the transversal crossing them. When the two lines are parallel, each pair is equal. This article covers the definition, the theorem and its converse, how to spot the pairs, and six worked examples.
- [Alternate Interior Angles Theorem: Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/alternate-interior-angles-theorem): The alternate interior angles theorem states that when a transversal crosses two parallel lines, each pair of alternate interior angles is congruent (equal). This article gives the formal statement, a full two-step proof, the converse and its proof, the related co-interior angles theorem, and six worked examples. For the underlying definition of the angle pair, see Alternate Interior Angles.
- [Alternate Interior Angles: Theorem & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/alternate-interior-angles): Alternate interior angles are the pair of angles that sit between two lines and on opposite sides of the line crossing them, called a transversal. When the two lines are parallel, each pair is equal. This article covers the definition, the theorem and its converse, how to spot the pairs, co-interior angles, and six worked examples.
- [Alternate Segment Theorem: Statement, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/alternate-segment-theorem): The alternate segment theorem states that the angle between a tangent to a circle and a chord drawn from the point of contact equals the inscribed angle that the chord subtends in the alternate segment. This article states the theorem precisely, proves it from the inscribed angle theorem, gives the converse, and works through six examples plus the mistakes students make.
- [Angle Angle Side (AAS) Congruence: Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angle-angle-side): Angle Angle Side (AAS) is a triangle congruence rule: if two angles and a non-included side of one triangle equal the corresponding two angles and side of another, the triangles are congruent. This article covers the statement, why it works, the proof from ASA, the difference between AAS and ASA, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Angle Bisector Theorem: Statement, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angle-bisector-theorem): The angle bisector theorem states that the bisector of an angle in a triangle divides the opposite side into two segments whose ratio equals the ratio of the two adjacent sides: BD/DC = AB/AC. This article covers the statement, the internal and external versions, a clean similar-triangles proof, the converse, the length-of-bisector formula, six worked examples, the common mistakes, and where the theorem leads next.
- [Angle Bisector: Properties & Construction](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angle-bisector): An angle bisector is a ray, line, or segment that divides an angle into two equal smaller angles. This article covers the definition, the key properties, the compass-and-straightedge construction, the angle bisector of a triangle and the incenter, and six worked examples.
- [Angle Side Angle (ASA): Rule, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angle-side-angle): The angle side angle (ASA) rule states that two triangles are congruent if two angles and the side included between them in one triangle equal the corresponding two angles and included side of the other — and the word included is what separates ASA from AAS. This article covers the statement, a full proof, two-column proof use, the ASA-versus-AAS difference, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Angle Sum Property in Quadrilaterals: 360° Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angle-sum-property-in-quadrilaterals): The angle sum property in quadrilaterals says the four interior angles of any quadrilateral add to 360°. This article proves it by drawing one diagonal to split the shape into two triangles, then applies it to find missing angles, covers concave and cyclic cases, and lists the common mistakes.
- [Angle Sum Property: Triangle, Quadrilateral, Polygon](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angle-sum-property): The angle sum property states that the interior angles of a triangle always add to 180°, the interior angles of a quadrilateral add to 360°, and the interior angles of any polygon with $n$ sides add to $(n-2)\times 180°$. This article proves each rule from a single idea, gives the formula with every variable defined, and works through six examples plus the mistakes students make.
- [Angles in Geometry: Definition, Types & Measurement](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angles): An angle is the figure formed when two rays share a common endpoint, and its size is the amount of turn between those rays, measured in degrees. This article covers the parts of an angle, the six types (acute, right, obtuse, straight, reflex, complete), how to measure one with a protractor, and the mistakes that trip students up.
- [Angles in a Pentagon: Interior, Exterior, and How to Find Them](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angles-in-a-pentagon): The interior angles of any pentagon always add to 540°, found with the formula (n − 2) × 180° where n = 5. In a regular pentagon each interior angle is 108° and each exterior angle is 72°. This article shows where 540° comes from, how to find a missing angle in an irregular pentagon, and the slips students make along the way.
- [Angles of a Parallelogram: Properties, Theorems, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angles-of-a-parallelogram): In a parallelogram, opposite angles are equal, adjacent (consecutive) angles add to 180°, and all four angles sum to 360°. This guide proves both theorems — opposite angles equal, adjacent angles supplementary — and uses them to find every missing angle, with worked examples, the underlying parallel-line reasoning, and the slips to avoid
- [Angles of a Rectangle - Properties and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angles-of-rectangle): Every one of a rectangle's four interior angles is 90°, and they add to 360°. This article explains why all four are right angles, what the diagonals do to those corners (they split each 90° into two acute angles and are equal but not perpendicular), and works through six examples plus the mistakes students make.
- [Angles of a Square - Interior, Diagonal, and Sum of Angles](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/angles-of-square): Every interior angle of a square measures exactly 90°, so the four angles sum to 360°. Its two diagonals cross at the centre at 90° and split each 90° corner into two 45° angles. This article proves each of these facts, works through examples, and separates the square's angle behaviour from that of a general rectangle or quadrilateral.
- [Applications of Integration: Area, Volume & Work](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/applications-of-integration): The applications of integration are the geometric and physical quantities a definite integral $\int_a^b f(x),dx$ can compute: area under a curve, area between two curves, volume of a solid of revolution, arc length, the average value of a function, work, and any accumulated change. Every one uses the same move: slice the quantity into infinitely thin pieces, write one integral for the piece, and add the pieces up. The integral is the total; the integrand is the size of one slice.
- [Arcs and Subtended Angles: Central & Inscribed](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/arcs-and-subtended-angles): An arc subtends an angle wherever two lines drawn to its endpoints meet. The central angle (vertex at the centre) is always twice the inscribed angle (vertex on the circle) that stands on the same arc - the Central Angle Theorem. This article covers arcs, subtended angles, the theorem's proof, key properties, and six worked examples.
- [Area Under a Curve: Definite Integral & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/area-under-a-curve): The area under a curve $y = f(x) \ge 0$ between $x = a$ and $x = b$ equals the definite integral $\int_a^b f(x),dx$, which is itself the limit of Riemann sums, the total area of thinner and thinner rectangles under the curve. When part of the curve dips below the x-axis, the plain integral returns signed (net) area; for total area you split the interval where $f$ crosses zero and integrate $\lvert f(x)\rvert$. You evaluate either one with the antiderivative: $\int_a^b f(x),dx = F(b) - F(a)$.
- [Area of Similar Triangles: Ratio Theorem, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/areas-of-similar-triangles): For two similar triangles, the ratio of their areas equals the square of the ratio of their corresponding sides. If the sides are in ratio $k$ (the scale factor), the areas are in ratio $k^2$: $\dfrac{\text{Area of } \triangle ABC}{\text{Area of } \triangle DEF} = \left(\dfrac{AB}{DE}\right)^2$. This guide states the theorem, proves it, and works six examples in both directions.
- [Area vs Perimeter – Differences Explained for Parents](https://bhanzu.com/math/area-vs-perimeter): This article clarifies the area vs perimeter distinction with plain definitions, a visual comparison, real-life examples, and the most common misconceptions children carry from classroom to exam. You will understand not just the difference in formula but the difference in what each measurement actually tells you about a shape.
- [Argand Plane - Definition, Modulus, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/argand-plane): The Argand plane is a coordinate plane that pictures a complex number $z = x + iy$ as the single point $(x, y)$, using a horizontal real axis and a vertical imaginary axis. This article shows how to plot complex numbers, read off the modulus $|z| = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ and argument, and how the Argand plane differs from an ordinary Cartesian plane.
- [Arithmetic And Geometric Progression: AP vs GP](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/arithmetic-and-geometric-progression): Arithmetic and geometric progression are the two building-block number patterns: an arithmetic progression (AP) adds a fixed common difference $d$ each step, while a geometric progression (GP) multiplies by a fixed common ratio $r$. That single choice, add or multiply, is why an AP grows in a straight line and a GP grows explosively, and it decides which nth-term and sum formula you reach for.
- [Axioms and Postulates: Meaning, Differences & Proofs](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/axioms-and-postulates): Axioms and postulates are statements geometry accepts as true without proof and then uses as the foundation for every proof that follows. This article explains what each one means, the historical difference between an axiom and a postulate, worked examples, and how theorems are built on top of them.
- [Basic Area Concepts: Unit Squares & Area of Shapes](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/basic-area-concepts): Area is the amount of flat space a shape covers, measured in square units by counting how many unit squares fit inside it. This article defines area, shows the unit-square method, derives the area formulas for squares, rectangles, and triangles, works six examples, and clears up the mistakes students make when area and perimeter get tangled.
- [Basic Proportionality Theorem: Statement, Proof & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/basic-proportionality-theorem): The Basic Proportionality Theorem (BPT), also called Thales' theorem, says that if a line is drawn parallel to one side of a triangle and cuts the other two sides, it divides those two sides in the same ratio: for $DE \parallel BC$, $\dfrac{AD}{DB} = \dfrac{AE}{EC}$. This guide gives the statement, the area-based proof, the converse, six worked examples, and the ratio-matching mistake that trips most students.
- [CPCTC: Meaning, Proof & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/cpctc): CPCTC stands for corresponding parts of congruent triangles are congruent: once two triangles are proven congruent, every matching pair of their sides and angles is automatically equal. This article covers what CPCTC means, why it follows from the definition of congruence, how it works as the final step of a two-column proof, six worked examples, the common mistakes, and where it leads next.
- [Cartesian Coordinate System: Definition, Quadrants, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/cartesian-coordinate-system): The Cartesian coordinate system pins down the exact position of any point using ordered pairs $(x, y)$ measured from two perpendicular axes that meet at the origin. This article defines the axes, the four quadrants and their sign rules, ordered pairs, how to plot points, and the 3D extension into octants.
- [Cartesian Plane: Definition, History & Quadrants](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/cartesian-plane): The Cartesian plane is the two-axis coordinate system invented by René Descartes that lets every point be named by an ordered pair $(x, y)$. This article covers who built it and why, how the plane is constructed from two perpendicular axes and an origin, the four quadrants, and worked examples of locating points.
- [Chords and Diameters: Definition & Properties](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/chords-and-diameters): A chord is a straight segment joining two points on a circle, and a diameter is the special chord that passes through the center, which makes it the longest chord of all, with length $2r$. This article covers the definitions, why the diameter always wins, the chord-length formula, the core chord properties, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Circle Theorems: The 8 Rules, Proofs & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/circle-theorems): Circle theorems are a set of eight rules linking the angles, chords, and tangents of a circle, and together they let you calculate missing angles without ever reaching for a protractor. This article states all eight, gives the short proof idea behind each, groups them so they are easy to recall, and works through mixed examples.
- [Circles Centered at the Origin: Equation & Graph](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/circles-centered-at-the-origin): A circle centered at the origin is the set of all points a fixed distance $r$ from $(0, 0)$, and every such circle obeys one clean equation: $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$. This article covers the equation, its derivation from the distance formula, how to graph it, how to recover the radius or the full equation from a single point, six worked examples, and the mistakes that trip students up.
- [Circles Touching Each Other: Internal & External Tangency](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/circles-touching-each-other): Two circles touch each other at exactly one point, and the giveaway is the distance between their centres: it equals the sum of the radii $R + r$ when they touch externally, and the difference $|R - r|$ when one touches the other from inside. This article gives both conditions, the collinear-centres property, how many common tangents each case allows, worked examples, and the mistakes to avoid.
- [Circumscribe: Circle, Polygon, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/circumscribe): To circumscribe means to draw one figure around another so it touches it. A circumscribed circle (circumcircle) passes through every vertex of a polygon; its centre is the circumcenter and its radius the circumradius. This article covers circumscribed circles and polygons, the formulas, and the difference from inscribed figures.
- [Classification of Triangles: By Sides & By Angles](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/classifying-triangles): The classification of triangles sorts every triangle two ways at once: by its sides (equilateral, isosceles, scalene) and by its angles (acute, right, obtuse). This article explains both frameworks, shows how they combine into named types like a right scalene triangle, lists the properties that decide each class, works six examples, and clears up where students misclassify.
- [Closed Shapes: Definition, Types & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/closed-shapes): A closed shape is a figure whose boundary starts and ends at the same point, leaving no gaps - like a square, circle, or triangle. This article explains closed shapes, how they differ from open shapes, the main types, their properties, and where you see them every day.
- [Coincident Lines: Definition, Condition & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/coincident-lines): Coincident lines are two lines that lie exactly on top of each other, sharing every point and therefore infinitely many solutions. This guide defines coincident lines, gives the coefficient-ratio condition $\frac{a_1}{a_2} = \frac{b_1}{b_2} = \frac{c_1}{c_2}$, separates them from parallel and intersecting lines, and works six examples
- [Collinear Points: Definition, How to Prove & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/collinear-points): Collinear points are three or more points that all lie on the same straight line. This article covers the definition, collinear versus non-collinear points, the three methods to prove collinearity (slope equality, zero triangle area, and the distance test), how each one works, and six worked examples.
- [Collinear Vectors — Definition, Conditions, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/collinear-vectors): Collinear vectors are vectors that lie along the same line — which means one is always a scalar multiple of the other, $\vec{a} = k,\vec{b}$. This article covers the three tests for collinearity (scalar multiple, equal coordinate ratios, zero cross product), how collinear differs from parallel and coplanar, and worked examples.
- [Common Tangents : Direct, Transverse, and How Many There Are](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/common-tangents): A common tangent is a line that touches two circles at the same time, and two circles can share anywhere from zero to four of them depending on how far apart they sit. This article separates direct from transverse tangents, gives the count for every configuration, derives the length formulas, and works through examples.
- [Compass Drawing - The Tool, Its Parts, And How To Use It](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/compass-drawing): A drawing compass is a hinged, two-legged instrument used to draw circles and arcs: one leg holds a sharp needle that pins the centre, the other holds a pencil that swings around it. The gap between the two legs sets the radius.
- [Complementary Angles — Definition, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/complementary-angles): Complementary angles are any two angles whose measures sum to exactly 90°. The two angles can sit side by side (forming a right angle — a corner) or be drawn anywhere on the page — what matters is the sum. This article covers the definition, properties, the two types (adjacent vs non-adjacent), the right-triangle connection, three worked examples.
- [Complete Angle : Definition, Measure, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/complete-angle): A complete angle is an angle that measures exactly 360° - one full rotation of a ray about its endpoint, back to where it started. It is also called a full angle, round angle, or perigon, equals $2\pi$ radians, and is four right angles. This article explains how a complete angle forms, how it compares to other angle types, and works through six examples.
- [Components of a Vector: Resolving Into i, j, k](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/components-of-a-vector): The components of a vector are the pieces it breaks into along each coordinate axis — the scalar components $x$, $y$, $z$ and the vector components $x\hat{i}$, $y\hat{j}$, $z\hat{k}$. Resolving a vector into components turns one slanted arrow into simple axis-aligned parts you can add and calculate with. This article covers 2D and 3D resolution, the formulas, and six worked examples.
- [Concave Polygons: Definition, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/concave-polygons): A concave polygon is a polygon with at least one reflex interior angle greater than 180°, which makes at least one vertex point inward and at least one diagonal fall outside the shape. This article defines concave polygons, contrasts them with convex ones, lists their properties, and works through examples like the arrowhead and the five-pointed star.
- [Concave Shape: Definition, Reflex Angle, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/concave-shapes-functions): A concave shape is one that caves inward: it has at least one interior angle greater than 180° (a reflex angle), and you can draw a straight line between two inside points that passes outside the shape. This guide explains the reflex-angle test and the diagonal test, covers concave 2D and 3D shapes, and works six examples.
- [Concurrent Lines: Definition, Conditions, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/concurrent-lines): Concurrent lines are three or more lines that all pass through a single common point, called the point of concurrency. This article defines them, shows the determinant condition for testing concurrency, walks through the four concurrency points of a triangle, and works six examples with the mistakes to avoid.
- [Concyclic Points: Definition, Conditions, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/concyclic-points): Concyclic points are points that all lie on one common circle, any two or three non-collinear points always are, but four points are concyclic only if they satisfy a condition, such as the opposite angles of the quadrilateral they form summing to $180°$. This article covers the definition, the conditions, three proof methods (angle, circle-equation, Ptolemy), six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Congruence in Triangles: Meaning, Rules, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/congruence-in-triangles): Congruence in triangles means two triangles are exactly equal in size and shape — every corresponding side and angle matches, even if one is flipped or turned. This article explains the meaning, the five tests (SSS, SAS, ASA, AAS, RHS), how the $\cong$ symbol and CPCTC work, with six worked examples and common mistakes
- [Congruent (Congruence) - Meaning, Definition, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/congruent): Congruent means identical in shape AND size. Two figures are congruent if one can be transformed into the other by rigid motions — translation, rotation, reflection — without stretching or shrinking. The symbol is $\cong$. For triangles, the five congruence theorems (SSS, SAS, ASA, AAS, RHS) let you prove congruence without measuring every side and angle.
- [Congruent Angles: Definition, Theorems, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/congruent-angles): Congruent angles are two or more angles that have the same measure, written $\angle A \cong \angle B$ using the congruence symbol ≅. This article covers the definition and symbol, the theorems that guarantee congruent angles, the compass-and-straightedge construction, and six worked examples.
- [Congruent Lines: Definition, Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/congruent-lines): Congruent lines are really line segments of equal length, written $\overline{AB} \cong \overline{CD}$ using the congruence symbol $\cong$. This article explains why true lines cannot be congruent, the three properties of congruent segments, how to check congruence with a ruler or the distance formula, and six worked examples.
- [Congruent Sides: Definition, Examples, How to Mark](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/congruent-sides): Congruent sides are two or more sides that have exactly the same length. This article explains what congruent sides mean, how tick marks signal them in a diagram, how they classify triangles (isosceles, equilateral) and quadrilaterals, with six worked examples and the mistakes to avoid
- [Consecutive Angles: Definition, Theorem & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/consecutive-angles): Consecutive angles are two angles that share a common side, following one after another around a figure, the adjacent angles at the two ends of the same side. In a parallelogram (and in co-interior pairs on parallel lines), consecutive angles are supplementary, summing to $180^\circ$. This guide covers the definition, the supplementary theorem, how consecutive angles differ from consecutive interior angles, and six worked examples.
- [Consecutive Interior Angles: Theorem & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/consecutive-interior-angles): Consecutive interior angles are the two non-adjacent interior angles that sit on the same side of a transversal, and the Consecutive Interior Angles Theorem says they are supplementary (sum to 180°) exactly when the two lines are parallel. This article covers the definition, the theorem, its converse for proving lines parallel, consecutive angles in a parallelogram, and six worked examples.
- [Constructing Angle Bisectors — Definition, Steps, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/constructing-angle-bisectors): Constructing an angle bisector means splitting an angle into two equal halves using only a compass and straightedge — no protractor. The method: draw an arc from the vertex cutting both rays, then two equal arcs from those cut points, and join the vertex to where they meet. This guide gives the exact steps, proves why they work, and shows the special case that builds a right angle.
- [Constructing Circles: Compass Steps and the 3-Point Method](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/constructing-circles): Constructing circles means drawing a circle with a compass from two pieces of information: a centre and a radius. This article shows the compass method for a given centre and radius, why two points allow infinitely many circles, and how three non-collinear points fix exactly one circle using perpendicular bisectors, with six worked examples and the mistakes to avoid.
- [Constructing Perpendicular Bisectors: Steps & Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/constructing-perpendicular-bisectors): Constructing a perpendicular bisector means using a compass and straightedge to draw the line that cuts a segment exactly in half at a right angle - no ruler measurements needed. This article gives the step-by-step construction with diagrams, proves why it works, lists the properties, and shows the common mistakes to avoid.
- [Constructing a 90 Degrees Angle: Compass Steps](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/constructing-an-angle-of-90-degrees): Constructing a 90 degrees angle with only a compass and straightedge means building a right angle from equal-radius arcs, no protractor needed. This article walks through the compass method step by step, shows why it produces exactly 90°, gives six worked constructions, and flags the errors that ruin the arc.
- [Constructing an Angle of 60 Degrees: Compass Steps](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/constructing-an-angle-of-60-degrees): Constructing an angle of 60 degrees with only a compass and straightedge means building one corner of an equilateral triangle, whose three angles are each 60°. Draw a base ray, sweep an arc from the endpoint, sweep an equal arc from where it crosses, and join the vertex to the intersection. This article shows the step-by-step image series, proves why it lands at exactly 60°, and works through six examples.
- [Construction of Angles : 30° to 120° With a Compass](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/construction-of-angle): The construction of angles uses only a compass and straightedge to draw exact angles without a protractor. A 60° angle comes from an equilateral-triangle arc, a 120° from two such arcs, a 90° from perpendicular arcs, and 30° and 45° come from bisecting 60° and 90°. This article gives step-by-step methods, the examples, and which angles are constructible.
- [Construction of Triangles: SSS, SAS, ASA, and RHS Methods](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/construction-of-triangles): Construction of triangles means drawing an exact triangle from given measurements using only a ruler and compass, and it works only when the data matches a congruence condition - SSS, SAS, ASA, or RHS. This article covers each method step by step, the special case of base and side-sum, why some data fails, and worked examples.
- [Converse of Pythagoras Theorem - Proof and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/converse-of-pythagoras-theorem): The converse of the Pythagoras theorem states that if the square of a triangle's longest side equals the sum of the squares of the other two - if a² + b² = c² - then the triangle is right-angled. This article gives the statement, a full proof, how the test also flags acute and obtuse triangles, and six worked examples plus common mistakes.
- [Convex Polygon: Definition, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/convex-polygon): A convex polygon is a polygon in which every interior angle is less than $180°$, so no corner caves inward and every diagonal stays inside the figure. This article defines the convex polygon, contrasts it with the concave polygon, gives three reliable tests to tell them apart, and works through examples and common mistakes.
- [Convex Shape - Definition, Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/convex-shapes-functions): A convex shape is a shape where the straight line joining any two points inside it stays completely inside - equivalently, every interior angle of a convex polygon is less than $180°$. This guide gives the one-line test, the properties, six worked examples, and the mistakes that make students misclassify a shape.
- [Coordinate Plane: Plot Points, Quadrants & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/coordinate-plane): A coordinate plane is the grid where every point is located by an ordered pair $(x, y)$ — the x-coordinate first, the y-coordinate second. This article shows how to plot a point, how to read a point's coordinates off a graph, the four quadrants and their sign rules, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Coplanar: Definition, Points, Lines & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/coplanar): Coplanar means lying on the same flat plane: a set of points is coplanar if a single plane can contain all of them, and lines are coplanar if one plane holds both. - [Corresponding Angles — Postulate, Pair Table, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/corresponding-angles): Corresponding angles are pairs of angles that occupy the same relative position at each of the two intersections formed when a transversal crosses two lines. When the two lines are parallel, corresponding angles are equal.
- [Corresponding Sides in Geometry - Definition, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/corresponding-sides): Corresponding sides are sides in the same relative position in two figures that are either similar or congruent. In congruent figures corresponding sides are equal; in similar figures they are proportional - their ratio is constant. This article shows how to match them from vertex order, how to use the ratio to find a missing length, and the mistakes to avoid
- [Cube Numbers — Definition, List, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/cube-numbers): A cube number is what you get when you multiply an integer by itself three times, such as $4^3 = 64$. - [Cube Root of 100 — Value and Steps](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/cube-root-of-100): The cube root of 100 is an irrational number equal to ∛100 ≈ 4.642, because 100 factors as 2² × 5² with no perfect cube inside it. - [Difference Between Cube and Cuboid](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/difference-between-cube-and-cuboid): The difference between a cube and a cuboid is the shape and size of the faces: a cube has six equal square faces and all edges the same length, while a cuboid has rectangular faces and can have three different edge lengths. Both have 6 faces, 12 edges, and 8 vertices, so every cube is a special cuboid. This article compares their properties, formulas, and worked examples.
- [Difference Between Kite and Rhombus](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/difference-between-kite-and-rhombus): The main difference between a kite and a rhombus is the sides: a rhombus has all four sides equal, while a kite has two pairs of adjacent equal sides of different lengths. - [Direction Fields: Slope Fields For Differential Equations](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/direction-fields): Direction fields (also called slope fields) are pictures of a first-order differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$: at a grid of points you draw a short segment whose slope is $f(x, y)$, and any solution curve threads through the field tangent to those segments everywhere. They let you read the qualitative behavior of solutions, where a constant equilibrium sits, whether it is stable, and where curves head in the long run, without ever solving the equation.

- [Eccentricity of Parabola: Why It Equals 1](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/eccentricity-of-parabola): The eccentricity of a parabola is exactly $e = 1$, because every point on a parabola is the same distance from the focus as from the directrix. The 7 axioms (Euclid's "common notions") are general truths about equality that apply across mathematics; the 5 postulates are assumptions specific to geometry, such as "a straight line can be drawn between any two points." This article lists all twelve, explains each, and clarifies how an axiom differs from a postulate.
- [Euclid's Fifth Postulate: The Parallel Postulate](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/euclids-fifth-postulate): Euclid's fifth postulate - the parallel postulate - says that if a line crossing two lines makes the interior angles on one side add to less than 180°, those two lines meet on that side. Its simpler equivalent (Playfair's axiom) is that through a point not on a line, exactly one parallel can be drawn. This article states the postulate precisely, explains why 2,000 years of attempted proofs failed, and shows how denying it created non-Euclidean geometry.

This article gives an overview of that axiomatic system, states the five postulates in brief, and shows how the fifth led to non-Euclidean geometry.
- [Exterior Angle Theorem: Statement, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/exterior-angle-theorem): The exterior angle theorem states that an exterior angle of a triangle equals the sum of the two interior angles not next to it: exterior angle = sum of the two remote interior angles. - [Icosahedron - Definition, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/icosahedron): An icosahedron is a three-dimensional solid with 20 equilateral-triangle faces, 30 edges, and 12 vertices, and it is one of the five Platonic solids. - [Inscribed Angle Theorem - Statement, Proof, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/inscribed-angle-theorem): The inscribed angle theorem states that an angle inscribed in a circle is half the central angle that subtends the same arc - so $\angle$inscribed $= \tfrac{1}{2}\angle$central. This article gives the exact statement, proves it in three cases, derives its main corollaries (angle in a semicircle is $90°$; angles on the same arc are equal), and works through examples.
- [Intersect in Math: Lines, Curves & Sets Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/intersect): To intersect means to cross or overlap - to share a common point or element. Two lines intersect at a single point of intersection, found by solving their equations together. Two curves intersect wherever their equations are equal, found by substitution. Two sets intersect in the elements they share, written $A \cap B$. This article covers all three senses of "intersect," gives the point-of-intersection formula for two lines with every variable named, works six examples, and shows why some lines never meet.

- [Is a Square a Rectangle? Yes — Here's Why](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/is-a-square-a-rectangle): Yes — every square is a rectangle, because a rectangle is defined as a quadrilateral with four right angles, and a square has those four right angles plus the extra condition that all its sides are equal. - [Isosceles Trapezoid: Properties, Area & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/isosceles-trapezoid): An isosceles trapezoid is a four-sided shape with one pair of parallel sides (the bases) and two non-parallel sides (the legs) of equal length, which gives it equal base angles, equal diagonals, and a line of symmetry. This article covers its definition, properties, the area formula $A = \tfrac{1}{2}(a+b)h$, perimeter, diagonals, six worked examples, and where students go wrong.
- [Isosceles Triangle Theorem: Proof, Converse, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/isosceles-triangle-theorem): The isosceles triangle theorem says that if two sides of a triangle are equal, then the angles opposite those sides — the base angles — are also equal. Its converse runs the other way: equal base angles force the opposite sides to be equal. This article gives the statement, a full proof using triangle congruence, the converse and its proof, and six worked examples.
- [Labelling Points in a Plane: Coordinates & Quadrants](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/labelling-points-in-a-plane): Labelling points in a plane means giving every point a unique address — an ordered pair $(x, y)$ — measured from the x-axis and y-axis of the coordinate plane. This article covers ordered pairs, the four quadrants and their signs, how to plot a point step by step, worked examples, and the order mistake that sends points to the wrong place.
- [Line Segment: Definition, Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/line-segment): A line segment is a part of a straight line bounded by two distinct endpoints, so it has a fixed, measurable length — unlike a line or a ray, which run on forever. This article covers the definition and notation, how a segment differs from a line and a ray, its properties, the distance formula for length, and six worked examples.
- [Line Symmetry — Definition, Lines of Symmetry, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/line-symmetry): Line symmetry means a figure can be folded along a straight line so the two halves match exactly, like a mirror image. This guide defines the line of symmetry, shows how to test for one by folding, gives the line counts for common shapes (square 4, rectangle 2, equilateral triangle 3, circle infinite), and works through examples and mistakes
- [Linear Pair of Angles: Definition & Axiom](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/linear-pair-of-angles): A linear pair of angles is two adjacent angles whose non-common sides form a straight line, so they always add to 180°. This article covers the definition, the linear pair axiom and its converse, how a linear pair differs from supplementary and vertical angles, and six worked examples.
- [Lines Of Symmetry In A Parallelogram - Why The Answer Is Zero](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/lines-of-symmetry-in-a-parallelogram): A general parallelogram has 0 lines of symmetry - there is no straight line you can fold it along so that the two halves match. Its diagonals split it into equal areas but not into mirror images, and no midline works either.
- [Lines Parallel to the Same Line: Theorem & Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/lines-parallel-to-the-same-line): If two lines are each parallel to the same line, then those two lines are parallel to each other. This is the transitive property of parallel lines, and this article gives the theorem statement, a transversal-and-corresponding-angles proof, its extension to three or more lines, worked examples, and the traps that make students doubt a rule they can actually see.
- [Lines and Angles: Types, Pairs & Properties Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/lines-and-angles-basic-terms): Lines and angles is the branch of geometry that studies straight paths and the openings they form where they meet, including parallel, perpendicular, and intersecting lines, and angle pairs such as linear pairs, vertically opposite, corresponding, and alternate angles. This article maps the types of lines, the angle pairs they create, and what happens when a transversal crosses parallel lines.
- [Lines in Geometry: Types & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/lines): Lines in geometry are straight, one-dimensional paths that have no thickness and run on forever in both directions. This article sorts out the point–line–ray–segment family first, then walks through every type a student meets: horizontal, vertical, parallel, perpendicular, intersecting, transversal, and skew lines, each with a labelled diagram and worked examples.
- [Lines of Symmetry in a Rectangle — Definition and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/lines-of-symmetry-in-a-rectangle): A rectangle has exactly 2 lines of symmetry: one vertical line through the midpoints of the longer sides and one horizontal line through the midpoints of the shorter sides. This guide shows why those two folds work, why the diagonals are not lines of symmetry, how a rectangle differs from a square, and how its rotational symmetry adds order 2
- [Locus in Maths: Definition, Equation, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/locus): A locus is the set of all points, and only those points, that satisfy a given condition. This article defines a locus, shows how a circle, a perpendicular bisector, and a parabola each emerge from one rule, derives their equations from the distance formula, and works through six examples plus the mistakes students make.
- [Measuring Angles: How to Use a Protractor Step by Step](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/measuring-angles): Measuring angles means finding the size of the opening between two rays, usually in degrees, by lining a protractor's centre on the vertex and reading the scale that starts at $0°$ on one arm. This article covers the protractor steps, how to read the inner and outer scale, measuring reflex angles, and the mistakes that produce wrong readings.
- [Methods to Draw a Line Segment: Ruler & Compass](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/methods-to-draw-a-line-segment): There are two methods to draw a line segment of a given length: the ruler method (mark both endpoints against a scale and join them) and the ruler-and-compass method (set the compass to the length, then transfer it as an arc). This article walks through both step by step with labelled figures, compares their accuracy, and works six examples plus the mistakes beginners make.
- [Midpoint Theorem - Statement, Proof, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/mid-point-theorem): The midpoint theorem states that the segment joining the midpoints of two sides of a triangle is parallel to the third side and exactly half its length. This article gives the formal statement, a full labelled proof, the converse, six worked examples, and the common mistakes, plus how the theorem relates to the triangle's midsegment.
- [Midsegment of a Triangle - Definition, Theorem, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/midsegment-of-a-triangle): The midsegment of a triangle is the segment joining the midpoints of two sides. By the midsegment theorem it is parallel to the third side and exactly half its length. This article defines the midsegment, proves the theorem, works through examples, and clarifies how the midsegment relates to (and differs from) the midpoint theorem.
- [Multiplication of a Vector by a Scalar : Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/multiplication-of-a-vector-by-a-scalar): Multiplication of a vector by a scalar stretches or shrinks the vector by the factor $|k|$ and keeps its direction when $k > 0$ or reverses it when $k < 0$. This article covers the component rule $k\vec{v} = (ka)\hat{i} + (kb)\hat{j}$, the effect on magnitude and direction, the algebraic properties, and six worked examples.
- [Negative Slope - Meaning, Graph, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/negative-slope): A negative slope means a line falls from left to right - as x increases, y decreases. It is found with $m = \dfrac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}$, and it comes out negative whenever the two variables move in opposite directions. This article covers what negative slope means, how to calculate it, its graph, real examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Nonagon: Sides, Angles, and Diagonals of a 9-Gon](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/nonagon): A nonagon is a 9-sided polygon (also called an enneagon or 9-gon) with 9 vertices and 9 angles. This article covers its interior angle sum of $1260^\circ$, the $140^\circ$ each angle of a regular nonagon, the $40^\circ$ exterior angle, its 27 diagonals, the regular-versus-irregular and convex-versus-concave types, and six worked examples.
- [Obtuse Angle: Definition, Degrees & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/obtuse-angle): An obtuse angle is any angle that measures more than $90°$ and less than $180°$ — wider than a right angle but not yet a straight line. This article defines the obtuse angle, lists its properties, shows where it appears in real life and in triangles, and clears up the mistakes that trip students up.
- [Obtuse Scalene Triangle: Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/obtuse-scalene-triangle): An obtuse scalene triangle is a triangle with one obtuse angle and all three sides of different lengths — combining the "obtuse" classification by angle with the "scalene" classification by side. - [Octahedron: Faces, Edges, Vertices & Properties](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/octahedron): An octahedron is a polyhedron with 8 faces, and the regular octahedron is a Platonic solid built from 8 equilateral triangles with 12 edges and 6 vertices. This article defines it, shows its properties, its volume and surface-area formulas, how it obeys Euler's formula $F - E + V = 2$, and worked examples.
- [Opposite Angles: Definition, Theorem &   Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/opposite-angles): Opposite angles are the pairs of non-adjacent angles formed when two straight lines intersect, and they are always equal in measure. - [Ordered Pair: Definition, (x, y) Notation & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/ordered-pair): An ordered pair is two numbers written $(x, y)$ in a fixed order that together name a single point, where the order matters: $(2, 4)$ and $(4, 2)$ are different points. This article covers the notation, how to plot an ordered pair, the equality rule, where order changes the answer, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Origins of Geometry: History from Egypt to Euclid](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/origins-of-geometry): The origins of geometry lie in practical land measurement: Egyptians resurveyed flooded Nile fields and Babylonians tracked areas long before the Greeks turned those recipes into proof-based mathematics. This article traces geometry from Egypt and Babylon through Thales, Pythagoras, and Euclid, and explains why it is called "earth measurement."
- [Orthocenter - Definition, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/orthocenter): The orthocenter is the single point where a triangle's three altitudes meet. It sits inside an acute triangle, on the right-angle vertex of a right triangle, and outside an obtuse triangle. This article shows how to find it by hand and on the coordinate plane, and how it differs from the circumcenter, incenter, and centroid.
- [Oval Shape: Definition, Properties, Oval vs Ellipse](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/oval-shape): An oval is a smooth, closed, curved 2D shape that looks like the outline of an egg — no straight sides, no corners, no vertices. The word is informal: it names a family of egg-like curves rather than one exact shape.
- [Pairs of Angles: Types, Definitions & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/pairs-of-angles): Pairs of angles are two angles linked by a measurement rule or a shared position, the main types being complementary (sum 90°), supplementary (sum 180°), adjacent, vertical, linear pair, and corresponding angles. - [Parallel & Perpendicular Lines: Slope Rules](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/parallel-and-perpendicular-lines): Parallel and perpendicular lines are two relationships you can read straight off slopes: parallel lines have equal slopes ($m_1 = m_2$), and perpendicular lines have slopes that multiply to −1 ($m_1 \cdot m_2 = -1$). This article covers both slope rules, why each works, how to tell lines apart from their equations, the special vertical-line case, and six worked examples.
- [Parallel Lines Cut by a Transversal: Angles, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/parallel-lines-cut-by-transversal): When two parallel lines are cut by a transversal, the crossing makes eight angles that fall into four named pairs — corresponding, alternate interior, alternate exterior, and co-interior angles — and the parallel condition forces each pair to be either equal or supplementary. - [Parallel Lines — Definition, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/parallel-lines): Parallel lines are two lines in the same plane that never meet, no matter how far they are extended. They have equal slopes, are equidistant at every point, and never share a common point. This article covers the definition, key properties, the transversal-angle relationships, three worked examples, and the role of parallel lines in Euclid's fifth postulate.
- [Parallel Vectors - Definition, Condition, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/parallel-vectors): Two vectors are parallel when one is a scalar multiple of the other: $\vec{a} = k,\vec{b}$. Equivalently, their cross product is the zero vector. This article covers the definition, the scalar and cross-product conditions, same-direction versus anti-parallel cases, worked examples, and the mistakes to avoid.
- [Parallelepiped: Definition, Volume & Scalar Triple Product](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/parallelepiped): A parallelepiped is a three-dimensional solid bounded by six parallelogram faces, with opposite faces parallel and congruent. Its volume equals the absolute value of the scalar triple product of the three edge vectors meeting at a corner: $V = |\vec{a} \cdot (\vec{b} \times \vec{c})|$. This article defines the solid, renders it with measurements, and works through the volume calculation step by step.
- [Parallelograms on the Same Base and Between the Same Parallels](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/parallelograms-same-base-same-parallels): Two parallelograms on the same base and between the same parallels are equal in area, even when one leans far more than the other. This article states the theorem, proves it with a pair of congruent triangles, draws out the "triangle is half the parallelogram" corollary, and works through six examples.
- [Parametric Equations: Slope, Arc Length & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/parametric-equations): Parametric equations describe a curve by giving both coordinates as functions of a third variable, a parameter $t$: $x = f(t)$ and $y = g(t)$. You can often eliminate $t$ to recover a Cartesian equation, and calculus works directly on the parameter: the slope is $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy/dt}{dx/dt}$ (whenever $\frac{dx}{dt} \neq 0$), and arc length is $L = \int_a^b \sqrt{(dx/dt)^2 + (dy/dt)^2}, dt$.

- [Pentagon Shape - Properties, Area, and Perimeter](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/pentagon-shape): A pentagon is a polygon with 5 sides and 5 interior angles summing to $540°$. A regular pentagon has all sides equal and all angles equal to $108°$ each. Its area formula is $A = \tfrac{1}{4}\sqrt{5(5 + 2\sqrt{5})} \cdot s^2 \approx 1.72 s^2$, and its perimeter is $P = 5s$.
- [Pentagonal Pyramid: Volume, Surface Area, Faces](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/pentagonal-pyramid): A pentagonal pyramid is a 3D solid with a pentagon base and five triangular faces meeting at a single apex, giving 6 faces, 10 edges, and 6 vertices. Its volume is $V = \dfrac{1}{3} \times (\text{base area}) \times h$ and its surface area adds the base to the five triangles. This guide derives both formulas and works six examples.
- [Pentagram: Definition, Angles & the Golden Ratio](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/pentagram): A pentagram is a five-pointed star formed by connecting every second vertex of a regular pentagon, written as the star polygon {5/2}. - [Perfect Cube — Definition, List, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/perfect-cube): A perfect cube is an integer of the form $n^3$ where $n$ is an integer — like $1, 8, 27, 64, 125$. - [Perpendicular Bisector: Definition & Construction](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/perpendicular-bisectors): A perpendicular bisector of a line segment is a line that cuts the segment into two equal halves and meets it at a right angle ($90°$). Every point on it is equidistant from the two endpoints. This article covers the definition, the properties, the equidistance theorem, the compass-and-straightedge construction, the circumcentre, and six worked examples.

- [Positive Slope: Definition, Graph & Worked Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/positive-slope): A positive slope describes a line that rises from left to right: as x increases, y increases too, so the slope value $m$ is greater than 0. - [Prisms in Geometry: Types, Volume, and Surface Area](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/prism): A prism is a 3D solid with two identical, parallel polygon bases joined by flat rectangular faces. - [Properties of Parallel Lines: Angles & Rules](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/properties-of-parallel-lines): The core properties of parallel lines appear when a transversal cuts them: corresponding angles are equal, alternate interior and alternate exterior angles are equal, and co-interior (same-side) angles are supplementary, adding to 180°. Each quadrant has a specific sign convention for $(x, y)$: Quadrant I: both positive; II: $x$ negative, $y$ positive; III: both negative; IV: $x$ positive, $y$ negative.
- [RHS Congruence Rule: Statement, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/rhs): The RHS congruence rule (Right angle, Hypotenuse, Side) states that two right triangles are congruent if the hypotenuse and one side of one equal the hypotenuse and the corresponding side of the other. - [Reflection Symmetry - Definition, Mirror Line, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/reflection-symmetry): Reflection symmetry exists when a single straight line - the mirror line, also called the line of symmetry - divides a shape into two halves that are exact mirror images of each other. Every point on one side has a matching point the same distance away on the other side.
- [Reflex Angle: Definition, Degree Range, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/reflex-angle): A reflex angle is any angle greater than $180°$ and less than $360°$ - the larger opening on the outside of an ordinary angle.
