- [Right Circular Cone: Volume, Surface Area, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/right-circular-cone): A right circular cone is a 3D solid with one circular base and a single apex that sits directly above the centre of that base, so its axis stands perpendicular to the base. Its volume is $\frac{1}{3}\pi r^2 h$, its curved surface area is $\pi r l$, and its total surface area is $\pi r(l + r)$, where $l$ is the slant height. This article derives each formula, explains exactly what makes a cone "right circular" rather than oblique, separates the height from the slant height, and works through examples and the slips students hit most
- [Right Circular Cylinder: Definition, Volume, Surface Area, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/right-circular-cylinder): A right circular cylinder is a 3D solid with two equal, parallel circular bases joined by a curved surface, where the axis joining the base centres stands perpendicular to the bases. Its volume is $\pi r^2 h$, its curved surface area is $2\pi r h$, and its total surface area is $2\pi r(h + r)$. This article derives each formula, explains exactly what makes a cylinder "right circular" rather than oblique, and works through examples and the slips students hit most
- [Right Rectangular Prism: Volume, Surface Area, and Diagonal](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/right-rectangular-prism): A right rectangular prism is a 3D solid with 6 rectangular faces, 12 edges, and 8 vertices, where every angle is a right angle, the shape most people call a cuboid. This article covers its properties, the volume $= l \times w \times h$, the surface area $= 2(lw + wh + lh)$, the space diagonal $= \sqrt{l^2 + w^2 + h^2}$, how it differs from a general rectangular prism, and six worked examples.
- [Right Scalene Triangle: Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/right-scalene-triangle): A right scalene triangle has one right angle (90°) and three sides all of different lengths, which also forces its three angles to be different. This article covers the definition, how a triangle can be right and scalene at once, the properties, the area and perimeter formulas with derivation, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Right-Angled Triangle Constructions: Steps & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/right-angled-triangle-constructions): Right-angled triangle constructions build a triangle with one $90^\circ$ angle using a ruler and compass, from either the hypotenuse and one leg (RHS), the two legs, or one leg and an acute angle. This article covers each case step by step, the properties that make them work, common mistakes, and six worked examples.
- [Rise Over Run - The Slope Method Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/rise-over-run): Rise over run is the step-by-step method for finding the slope of a line: count the vertical change (rise, $\Delta y$) and divide it by the horizontal change (run, $\Delta x$). It turns "how steep is this line?" into a fraction you can read straight off a graph or compute from two points. This guide gives the method, six worked examples, and the mistakes that flip the sign or the fraction.
- [Rotational Symmetry - Order & Angle of Rotation](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/rotational-symmetry): Rotational symmetry means a shape maps exactly onto itself when turned about its centre by some angle smaller than a full turn. The order counts how many times it matches in one $360°$ rotation, and the angle of rotation is $\dfrac{360°}{\text{order}}$; this guide gives the definitions, six worked examples, and the traps that produce a wrong order.
- [SAS Criterion in Triangles: Proof, Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/sas-criterion): The SAS criterion in triangles states that if two sides and the included angle of one triangle equal the two sides and included angle of another, the triangles are congruent. This article gives the rule, its transformation-based proof, worked examples, and the key reason SAS works while SSA does not.
- [SSA Congruence Rule: Why It Is Not Valid (Ambiguous Case)](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/ssa-congruence-rule): The SSA congruence rule (two sides and a non-included angle) is not generally valid, because the same SSA data can produce zero, one, or two different triangles, the ambiguous case. This article explains why the SSA congruence rule fails, shows the two-triangle construction, and covers the one right-angle (RHS / HL) exception where it does work.
- [SSS Criterion Proof: How Three Sides Fix a Triangle](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/the-sss-criterion-proof): The SSS criterion proof places one triangle's base on the other's, joins the far vertices, and uses the isosceles-triangle property twice to show the included angles are equal - then SAS finishes it. This article gives the formal statement, the step-by-step proof, Euclid's version, the rigid-motion view, and worked examples.
- [SSS Criterion in Triangles: The Rule and When to Use It](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/sss-criterion-in-triangles): The SSS criterion in triangles states that if the three sides of one triangle equal the three sides of another, the triangles are congruent — no angle check needed. This article covers the rule, when to reach for it, how it differs from SSS similarity, why SSA fails, and six worked examples.
- [Same Base and Same Parallels: Theorem & Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/same-base-same-parallels): The same base and same parallels theorem: figures sharing a base between the same parallels have equal area. See both cases and the corollaries.
- [Same Side Interior Angles: Theorem & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/same-side-interior-angles): Same side interior angles are the two angles that lie between two lines and on the same side of the transversal, and when the two lines are parallel they are supplementary (their sum is 180°). This article covers the definition, the same side interior angles theorem and its converse, why they are supplementary, and six worked examples that solve for the supplementary angle.
- [Scale in Maths: Scale Drawings and Map Scale](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/scale): In maths, scale is the ratio between a length on a drawing or map and the matching length in real life, written like 1 cm : 5 km or 1 : 50,000. This article covers what scale means, how to read a map scale, how to convert between map distance and real distance in both directions, six worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Sector of a Circle: Area, Arc Length, Perimeter](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/sector-of-a-circle): A sector of a circle is the pie-slice region enclosed by two radii and the arc between them, and its area is the fraction of the whole circle set by its central angle: Area = (θ/360°) × πr². This article covers the definition, minor and major sectors, the area, arc length, and perimeter formulas in both degrees and radians, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Segment Bisector: Definition, Types & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/segment-bisector): A segment bisector is any point, line, ray, or segment that passes through the midpoint of a line segment and splits it into two equal halves. This article covers the definition, the four kinds of bisector, how the perpendicular bisector is the special $90°$ case, the midpoint formula for finding one, and six worked examples.
- [Shapes in Geometry — Complete 2D and 3D Taxonomy](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/shapes): Geometric shapes split into 2D (flat, with length and width) and 3D (solid, with length, width, and height). 2D shapes group into polygons (triangles, quadrilaterals, regular and irregular) and non-polygons (circle, ellipse). 3D shapes group into polyhedra (prisms, pyramids, Platonic solids) and curved solids (sphere, cylinder, cone).
- [Side Angle Side (SAS) — Congruence and Similarity Rules](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/sas): Side Angle Side (SAS) is one criterion that does two jobs: two sides and the included angle prove triangles congruent when the sides are equal, and similar when the sides are proportional. This article keeps the two apart — same angle condition, different side condition — with a labelled diagram and proof for each, plus worked examples and the mistakes that blur them.
- [Side Side Side (SSS): Congruence Proof & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/sss): The side side side (SSS) rule states that if the three sides of one triangle equal the three sides of another, the triangles are congruent — with no angle information required at all. This article covers the statement, why three sides lock a triangle's shape, the proof, SSS similarity, six worked examples, the common mistakes, and where the rule leads next.
- [Similar Figures: Definition, Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/what-is-similarity-figures): Similar figures have the same shape but not necessarily the same size, their corresponding angles are equal and their corresponding sides are in a constant ratio called the scale factor, written with the symbol $\sim$. This guide covers the definition, the properties, the triangle-similarity criteria (AA, SSS, SAS), how similarity differs from congruence, why areas scale by the square of the ratio, and six worked examples.
- [Similar Triangles: Theorems & Properties](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/similar-triangles): Similar triangles are triangles with the same shape but not necessarily the same size: their corresponding angles are equal and their corresponding sides are in the same ratio. This article covers the definition, the AA, SAS, and SSS similarity criteria, the properties, the area-ratio rule, the difference from congruent triangles, six worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Skew Lines: Definition, Distance & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/skew-lines): Skew lines are two straight lines in three-dimensional space that never intersect and are never parallel, because they lie in different planes. This article covers the definition, why skew lines exist only in 3D, how to spot them in a cube, the conditions that classify two lines, the distance formula, and six worked examples.
- [Skew Symmetric Matrix - Properties, Theorems, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/skew-symmetric-matrix): A skew symmetric matrix is a square matrix that equals the negative of its transpose, so its diagonal entries are all zero and each entry is the negative of its mirror across the diagonal. This article covers the definition, why the diagonal must be zero, the properties, the symmetric-plus-skew decomposition theorem, the odd-order determinant rule, and six worked examples.
- [Slope Intercept Form: y = mx + b & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/slope-intercept-form-of-a-line): Slope intercept form is the equation of a straight line written as $y = mx + b$, where $m$ is the slope and $b$ is the y-intercept. This article covers how to read $m$ and $b$ straight off the equation, how to find them from points or a graph, how to convert other forms, and six worked examples.
- [Slope of Perpendicular Lines: The −1 Rule](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/slope-of-perpendicular-lines): The slope of perpendicular lines follows one rule: the product of the two slopes is −1, so $m_1 \cdot m_2 = -1$. This article covers the negative-reciprocal rule, why it holds, how to find a perpendicular slope from any given slope, the vertical-horizontal exception, and six worked examples.
- [Special Parallelograms: Rectangle, Rhombus, Square](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/special-parallelograms): The three special parallelograms are the rectangle, the rhombus, and the square. Each is a parallelogram with an extra condition: a rectangle adds four right angles, a rhombus adds four equal sides, and a square adds both. This article covers their shared properties, differences, and worked examples.
- [Sphere: Volume, Surface Area, and Great Circle Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/sphere): For a sphere of radius $r$, the volume is $\frac{4}{3}\pi r^3$ and the surface area is $4\pi r^2$ - exactly four times the area of its great circle ($\pi r^2$). Every flat slice through a sphere is a circle, and the biggest such slice, through the centre, is the great circle.
- [Square: Properties, Area, Perimeter, Diagonal](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/square): A square is a quadrilateral with four equal sides and four right angles — the most regular four-sided shape there is. This article covers its properties and the three formulas, all derived from the shape itself: area $A = s^2$, perimeter $P = 4s$, and diagonal $d = s\sqrt{2}$, with six worked examples and the mistakes students make most.
- [Straight Angle — Definition, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/straight-angle): A straight angle is an angle that measures exactly 180°, formed when its two arms point in opposite directions from a shared vertex and line up into a straight line. This article defines the straight angle, lists its properties, separates it from a straight line, and works through examples involving supplementary pairs and triangles.
- [Straight Line: Definition, Properties & Slope](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/straight-line): A straight line is a one-dimensional figure that extends infinitely in both directions, has no curves, and keeps a constant slope throughout. This article covers the definition and properties, what slope means and how to find it, a brief tour of the equation forms, and six worked examples.
- [Sum Of Exterior Angles Of A Triangle — Why It Is Always 360°](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/sum-of-exterior-angles-of-triangle): The sum of the exterior angles of a triangle is always $360°$ — taking one exterior angle at each of the three vertices. This holds for every triangle, whether it is scalene, isosceles, equilateral, acute, right, or obtuse.
- [Supplementary Angles — Definition, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/supplementary-angles): Supplementary angles are any two angles whose measures sum to exactly 180°. The two angles can be next to each other (forming a straight line — a linear pair) or completely separate — what matters is the sum, not the position.
- [Supplementary vs Complementary Angles — A Side-by-Side Comparison](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/supplementary-vs-complementary-angles): Supplementary angles add to $180°$; complementary angles add to $90°$. This article compares the two side by side, gives three worked examples (Quick, Standard, Stretch), explains the most common mistake, and offers the mnemonic that stops students mixing them up — Complementary forms a Corner, Supplementary forms a Straight line.
- [Symmetric Matrix — Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/symmetric-matrix): A symmetric matrix is a square matrix that equals its own transpose, $A^T = A$, so the entry in row $i$, column $j$ always matches the entry in row $j$, column $i$. This article covers the definition, the key properties (real eigenvalues, orthogonal eigenvectors, symmetric inverse), the symmetric-versus-skew-symmetric distinction, the decomposition theorem, and six worked examples.
- [Symmetric Property — Definition, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/symmetric-property): The symmetric property of equality says that if one quantity equals another, then the second equals the first — if $a = b$, then $b = a$. This article covers the formal definition, the congruence form, three worked examples at Quick/Standard/Stretch tiers, and where the symmetric property breaks (notably for inequalities).
- [Symmetry in Geometry - Types, Definition, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/symmetry): Symmetry in geometry means a shape looks identical after being transformed — moved, rotated, or flipped. There are three core types: reflection symmetry (mirror image across a line), rotational symmetry (looks the same after rotation by a fixed angle), and point symmetry (every point has a matching point through a central point)
- [Symmetry of a Circle - Lines and Rotational Symmetry](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/symmetry-of-any-circle): The symmetry of a circle is unlimited: a circle has infinitely many lines of symmetry - every line through the centre (each diameter) - and infinite rotational symmetry, because it looks the same after a turn of any angle about its centre. This article shows why, counts the lines, and clears up the common mix-ups.
- [Tangents From an External Point: Proof & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/tangents-from-an-external-point): From any point outside a circle you can draw exactly two tangents, and the theorem says their lengths are always equal. This article states the result, proves it with RHS congruence, lists the properties that follow (the line to the centre bisects the angle between the tangents), gives the length formula $\sqrt{OP^2 - r^2}$, and works through examples.
- [Tangents and Normals: Definition, Equations, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/tangents-and-normals): A tangent is a line that touches a curve at a single point with the same slope as the curve there, and a normal is the line through that point perpendicular to the tangent. Their slopes satisfy $m_{\text{tangent}} \cdot m_{\text{normal}} = -1$. This article covers both equations, their properties, the circle case, and six worked examples.
- [Tetrahedron: Faces, Edges, Vertices, Volume, and Surface Area](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/tetrahedron): A tetrahedron is a 3D solid with 4 triangular faces, 6 edges, and 4 vertices — the simplest possible polyhedron. A regular tetrahedron (all faces equilateral) has volume $\frac{\sqrt{2}}{12}a^3$ and total surface area $\sqrt{3},a^2$, where a is the edge length. This article covers its faces-edges-vertices count, derives the volume and surface-area formulas, shows its net, and works through examples.
- [Transitive Property of Congruence: Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/transitive-property-of-congruence): The transitive property of congruence states that if one figure is congruent to a second and the second is congruent to a third, then the first is congruent to the third — in symbols, if a ≅ b and b ≅ c, then a ≅ c. This article covers the statement for segments, angles, and triangles, where it differs from the substitution property, two-column proofs that use it, six worked examples, and the mistakes to watch for.
- [Transversal — All 8 Angles and Pair Relationships](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/transversal): A transversal is a line that crosses two or more other lines at distinct points. When the transversal crosses two parallel lines, exactly $8$ angles form — grouped into four named pair-relationships (corresponding, alternate interior, alternate exterior, co-interior).
- [Transversals and Related Angles: Types & Rules](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/transversals-and-related-angles): A transversal is a line that crosses two or more other lines, creating eight angles whose relationships fall into named pairs: corresponding, alternate interior, alternate exterior, and co-interior angles. This article shows each pair, the single rule that decides whether they are equal or supplementary when the crossed lines are parallel, worked examples, and the mistakes that cost marks.
- [Trapezium - Definition, Properties, Area and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/trapezium): A trapezium (US: trapezoid) is a quadrilateral with one pair of parallel sides. The parallel sides are called bases; the non-parallel sides are legs. The area formula is $A = \tfrac{1}{2}(a + b) \cdot h$ — the average of the parallel sides times the height.
- [Triangle Congruence Theorem — SSS, SAS, ASA, AAS, RHS](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/triangle-congruence-theorem): A triangle congruence theorem is a shortcut rule that confirms two triangles are identical in shape and size by checking only three matching parts instead of all six. This article walks through the five rules — SSS, SAS, ASA, AAS, and RHS — with a labelled diagram for each, worked examples, the AAA and SSA cases that fail, and how to pick the right rule fast.
- [Triangle Inequality Theorem: Rule and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/triangle-inequality-theorem): The triangle inequality theorem states that the sum of the lengths of any two sides of a triangle is always greater than the third side: $a + b > c$, $b + c > a$, and $a + c > b$. This article explains the rule, proves it, shows how to test three lengths, and works six examples with the common errors to avoid.
- [Triangles on the Same Base and Between the Same Parallels](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/triangles-same-base-same-parallels): Two triangles on the same base and between the same parallels are equal in area, because they share the same base and the same height. This article proves it two ways, links each triangle to half of a parallelogram, shows why a median splits a triangle evenly, and works six examples.
- [Triangles: Definition, Types, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/triangles): A triangle is a closed two-dimensional shape with three sides, three angles, and three vertices, and its interior angles always add to $180°$. This guide covers a triangle's parts, the six types (by side and by angle), the core properties, area and perimeter formulas, six worked examples, and common mistakes.
- [Triangular Pyramid: Volume, Surface Area & Faces](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/triangular-pyramid): A triangular pyramid, also called a tetrahedron, is a 3D solid with 4 triangular faces, 6 edges, and 4 vertices — the simplest possible polyhedron. This article covers its properties, the volume formula $V = \tfrac{1}{3} \times \text{base area} \times \text{height}$, the surface area formulas, the net, six worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Types of Angles — Acute, Right, Obtuse, Reflex](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/acute-angle): An acute angle measures between $0°$ and $90°$ — but it is only one of five named angle types. This article gives a complete reference table for all five (acute, right, obtuse, straight, reflex), with definitions, diagrams, real-world examples, and the common mistakes students make when sorting them.
- [Types of Angles: Acute, Right, Obtuse, Reflex](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/types-of-angles): The six types of angles by measure are acute (under 90°), right (exactly 90°), obtuse (90° to 180°), straight (180°), reflex (180° to 360°), and full (360°). This article defines each type with a diagram, then covers the angle-pair relationships — complementary, supplementary, adjacent, and vertical — and the common mistakes.
- [Types of Polygon: Classification with Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/types-of-polygon): Polygons are classified along four independent axes: by number of sides (triangle, pentagon, hexagon), by regularity (regular vs irregular), by shape (convex vs concave), and by structure (simple vs complex). This article works through every type with properties, a comparison table, examples, and the classification mistakes to avoid.
- [Types of Quadrilaterals: Definition and Classification](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/types-of-quadrilaterals): A quadrilateral is any four-sided closed shape whose interior angles sum to $360°$, and the main types of quadrilaterals are the parallelogram, rectangle, square, rhombus, trapezoid, and kite. This article defines each type, shows how they nest in one family tree, and works through examples of classifying a shape from its sides, angles, and diagonals.
- [Types of Relations - Reflexive, Symmetric, Transitive](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/types-of-relations): The main types of relations in math are empty, universal, identity, inverse, reflexive, symmetric, transitive, antisymmetric, and equivalence relations. This article defines each type, shows it with set examples written in ${\ldots}$ notation, explains how equivalence and antisymmetric relations differ, and works through six problems.
- [Types of Triangles — Classification Matrix](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/types-of-triangle): Triangles are classified two ways — by **side lengths** (equilateral, isosceles, scalene) and by **angle measures** (acute, right, obtuse). Combining the two axes gives a $3 \times 3$ matrix with **seven** valid types and two impossible ones. This article gives the complete matrix, properties of each type, three worked examples, and the impossibilities that come from the triangle angle-sum theorem.
- [Types of Vectors: Zero, Unit, Parallel & More](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/types-of-vectors): The main types of vectors are the zero vector (magnitude 0), the unit vector (magnitude 1), and vectors classified by how they relate to one another: equal, negative, parallel, collinear, coplanar, and position vectors. This article defines each type, shows the diagram for it, and works through six examples of spotting the type from the numbers.
- [Undefined Slope: Definition, Equation & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/undefined-slope): An undefined slope is the slope of a vertical line, where every point shares the same x-coordinate, so the slope formula divides by zero. This article covers why a vertical line's slope is undefined, the equation x = a, the graph, how undefined slope differs from zero slope, and six worked examples.
- [Understanding Who Invented Geometry and Its Impact on Daily Life and Science](https://bhanzu.com/math/understanding-who-invented-geometry-and-its-impact-on-daily-life-and-science): Geometry evolved from ancient civilizations, but Euclid systematized it into a structured way of thinking still used today. This guide helps parents bring his ideas to life through hands-on activities that build reasoning and spatial skills in kids.
- [Unit Circle: Definition, Coordinates, Chart, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/unit-circle): The unit circle is the circle of radius 1 centred at the origin, where every point on the rim has coordinates (cos θ, sin θ) for the angle θ measured from the positive x-axis. This article covers the definition, the equation x² + y² = 1, why the coordinates are cosine and sine, the special angles in both degrees and radians, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.

- [Vector Quantities: Definition, Examples & Scalar Difference](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/vector-quantities): A vector quantity is any quantity that needs both a magnitude and a direction to be fully described, like velocity or force. - [Vectors Triangle Inequality : Statement & Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/vectors-triangle-inequality): The vectors triangle inequality states that for any two vectors, $|\vec{a} + \vec{b}| \le |\vec{a}| + |\vec{b}|$ - the length of a sum never exceeds the sum of the lengths. This article states the full two-sided form, proves it two ways, pins down exactly when equality holds, and works six examples.
- [Vectors in Math — Definition, Types, and Worked Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/vectors): A vector is a quantity with both magnitude and direction, drawn as an arrow whose length is the size and whose arrowhead is the direction. This article covers the types of vectors, how to write them in component form, the core operations (addition, scalar multiples, dot and cross products), and the errors students hit most.
- [Vertical Angles — Definition, Theorem, Proof, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/vertical-angles): Vertical angles (also called vertically opposite angles) are the pair of non-adjacent angles formed when two straight lines cross at a single point. Sitting opposite each other across the intersection, they share only a vertex — never a side. The Vertical Angles Theorem states that vertical angles are always congruent (equal in measure), no matter how the two lines are oriented.
- [Vertical Line — Equation, Slope, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/vertical-line): A vertical line is a straight line where every point shares the same x-coordinate, so its equation is x = a and its slope is undefined (the run is zero, and you cannot divide by zero). This article covers the definition, the equation, why the slope is undefined, the vertical line test, six worked examples, and the errors students make most.
- [What is Adjacent? Meaning, Adjacent Angles, Solved Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/adjacent): In geometry, adjacent means "next to each other" — sharing a common side, edge, or vertex. Adjacent angles share a vertex and a side but don't overlap. Adjacent sides in a polygon share a common vertex. Adjacent in a triangle (with respect to an angle) is the side touching the angle that isn't the hypotenuse.
- [X and Y Axis in a Graph: Origin & Quadrants](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/x-and-y-axis): In a graph, the x-axis is the horizontal number line and the y-axis is the vertical number line; they cross at the origin, the point (0, 0). This article explains the two axes, the four quadrants and their sign conventions, and how to plot any point from its ordered pair $(x, y)$, with six worked examples.
- [X and Y Graph: How to Plot Points on the XY-Plane](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/x-and-y-graph): An x and y graph is a flat grid built from a horizontal x-axis and a vertical y-axis, where every point is named by an ordered pair $(x, y)$. This article shows how to plot points, why the x-coordinate comes first, what the four quadrants mean, and works through six plotting examples.
- [Zero Slope: Definition, Equation, Graph & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/zero-slope): A zero slope is the slope of a horizontal line, where the rise is 0, so the line is perfectly flat and its equation is y = c. This guide shows you how zero slope is calculated, how it looks on a graph, how it differs from an undefined slope, and the mistakes students make most often.
- [y = mx + b: Read Slope & Y-Intercept, Plot a Line](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/y-mx-b): In the equation $y = mx + b$, the number $m$ is the slope (how steeply the line rises or falls) and the number $b$ is the y-intercept (where the line crosses the y-axis). This article shows how to read $m$ and $b$ straight off the equation, plot the line, write the equation from points or a graph, and rearrange any linear equation into this form.

## Algebra Articles

- [Absolute Value Function — Graph, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/absolute-value-function): The absolute value function $f(x) = |x|$ outputs the distance of $x$ from zero — always non-negative. Its graph is a V centred at the origin. This article covers the piecewise definition, the V-shape, transformations $f(x) = a|x - h| + k$, three worked examples, and the most common slip in solving absolute-value equations.
- [Absolute Value Functions: Graph, Derivative & Integral](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/absolute-value-functions): Absolute Value Functions measure distance from zero, so $f(x) = |x|$ equals $x$ when $x \ge 0$ and $-x$ when $x < 0$. The graph is a V with its vertex at the origin, domain all real numbers, and range $y \ge 0$. In calculus the surprise is that $|x|$ is continuous everywhere yet not differentiable at $x = 0$, where the V has a sharp corner; away from that corner its derivative is $\operatorname{sgn}(x)$ and its integral is $\dfrac{x|x|}{2} + C$.
- [Absolute Value Inequalities - How to Solve and Graph Them](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/absolute-value-inequalities): An absolute value inequality compares a distance to a number, so it always breaks into two cases: $|x| < a$ means $x$ sits *between* $-a$ and $a$, while $|x| > a$ means $x$ sits outside that range. This article covers what these inequalities mean, the "and" versus "or" rule, how each looks on a number line, six worked examples, and the mistakes that cost the most marks.
- [Absolute Value: Definition, Symbol, Graph & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/absolute-value): The absolute value of a number is its distance from zero on the number line, written with vertical bars as $|x|$, and it is never negative. So $|5| = 5$ and $|-5| = 5$.
- [Accuracy In Maths: Rounding & Error Guide](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/accuracy): Accuracy in maths measures how close a value is to the true or accepted value. It is controlled by three tools: significant figures, rounding to a stated degree of accuracy, and error (absolute and relative). Accuracy is not the same as precision, and a wrong-by-a-little answer is still a wrong answer.
- [Adding Exponents - Rules, Methods, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/adding-exponents): Adding exponents follows two rules — only like terms combine, and exponents themselves don't add. Worked examples for same base, different bases, and fractional powers.
- [Adding Polynomials: Rules, Methods, Worked Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/adding-polynomials): Adding polynomials means combining like terms: terms with the same variable raised to the same power. You can add them horizontally, grouping like terms in a line, or vertically, stacking them in columns by power. This article covers the like-terms rule, both methods step by step, what happens with unlike terms, the sign and exponent traps that cost marks, and six worked examples from two binomials to multivariable polynomials.
- [Adding and Subtracting Polynomials: Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/adding-and-subtracting-polynomials): Adding and subtracting polynomials means combining like terms, terms that share the same variable raised to the same power. To add, drop the brackets and add the coefficients of matching terms; to subtract, flip the sign of every term in the second polynomial first, then combine. The result is written in standard form, from the highest power down to the constant.
- [Addition And Subtraction Of Algebraic Expressions](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/addition-and-subtraction-of-algebraic-expressions): Addition and subtraction of algebraic expressions comes down to one skill: combine only like terms, those with the same variables raised to the same powers. To add, you add the coefficients of like terms; to subtract, you flip the sign of every term being subtracted and then add. Both work the same way whether you line the terms up horizontally or stack them vertically in columns.
- [Addition Of Algebraic Expressions: Methods](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/addition-of-algebraic-expressions): Addition of algebraic expressions means combining like terms, terms with the same variables raised to the same exponents, by adding only their number coefficients while the variable part stays untouched. You can do it two ways: the horizontal method (rewrite everything on one line and group like terms) or the column method (stack like terms in vertical columns and add down).
- [Addition Property of Equality — Definition and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/addition-property-of-equality): The addition property of equality states that if $a = b$, then $a + c = b + c$ — adding the same value to both sides of an equation keeps it true. This article covers the formal rule, three worked examples from Quick to Stretch, the mistakes that cost marks, and the people who codified the axiom.
- [Addition of Matrices: Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/addition-of-matrices): Addition of matrices combines two matrices of the same order by adding the numbers in matching positions, so a sum exists only when both matrices have identical dimensions. The result keeps that same order, and the operation is commutative and associative, with the zero matrix as the additive identity.
- [Additive Identity Vs Multiplicative Identity Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/additive-identity-vs-multiplicative-identity): Additive identity vs multiplicative identity comes down to two numbers that leave a value untouched: the additive identity is $0$, because $a + 0 = a$, and the multiplicative identity is $1$, because $a \times 1 = a$. Zero is the "do nothing" number for addition; one is the "do nothing" number for multiplication. The two are never interchangeable, and the difference explains why $0$ has an additive partner but no multiplicative one.
- [Algebra 1 - What It Covers, Topics, Skills, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/algebra-1): Algebra 1 is the foundational course where numbers give way to variables, and students learn to write, solve, and graph equations, inequalities, and functions. This article covers what Algebra 1 is, the grade it is usually taken, the full topic list, the skills it builds, whether it is hard, and worked examples of the problems it asks.
- [Algebra 2: Topics, Concepts & How They Connect](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/algebra-2): Algebra 2 is the high-school course that extends Algebra 1 into the full world of functions (polynomial, rational, radical, exponential, logarithmic, and trigonometric) and adds matrices, sequences, and conic sections. The thread tying Algebra 2 together is the idea of a function: a rule that takes an input and returns a single output.
- [Algebraic Expression - Parts, Types, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/algebraic-expression): An algebraic expression is a mathematical phrase combining variables, constants, and operations — but with no equals sign. This article covers the formal definition, every part, the four types, simplification rules, the most common student mistakes, and where the entities live in modern math.
- [Algebraic Identities — List, Proofs, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/algebraic-identities): Algebraic identities are equations that stay true for every value of their variables — for example $(a+b)^2 = a^2 + 2ab + b^2$ and $a^2 - b^2 = (a+b)(a-b)$. This article gives you the full standard list (square, cube, and three-variable identities), a geometric and an algebraic proof, six worked examples, and the slips that cost the most marks.
- [Alternating Series Test: Leibniz Rule & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/alternating-series-test): The Alternating Series Test, also called the Leibniz Test, says that a series whose signs flip, $\sum (-1)^{n} b_n$ or $\sum (-1)^{n+1} b_n$ with every $b_n > 0$, converges when two things hold: the terms $b_n$ are eventually decreasing, and $\lim_{n\to\infty} b_n = 0$. When both are true, the sum settles on a finite value, and the error from stopping at the $N$th term is never larger than the first term you left out, $\lvert S - S_N\rvert \le b_{N+1}$.
- [Alternating Series: Test, Convergence & Error Bound](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/alternating-series): An alternating series is a sum whose terms flip sign, written $\sum (-1)^n b_n$ or $\sum (-1)^{n+1} b_n$ with $b_n \ge 0$. The Alternating Series Test says such a sum converges when the sizes $b_n$ decrease and shrink to zero: $b_{n+1} \le b_n$ and $\lim_{n \to \infty} b_n = 0$. The alternating harmonic series $1 - \tfrac12 + \tfrac13 - \tfrac14 + \cdots$ converges to $\ln 2$, and if you stop after $N$ terms the error is no bigger than the first term you dropped, $\lvert R_N \rvert \le b_{N+1}$.
- [Antilog Table: How to Read and Use It (With Examples)](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/antilog-table): An antilog table reverses a logarithm: given $\log x = y$, it recovers $x = \text{antilog}(y)$. This article shows how to split a logarithm into its characteristic and mantissa, read the row, column, and mean-difference values, place the decimal point, and handle negative (bar) characteristics.
- [Applications Of Quadratic Equations: Real Uses](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/applications-of-quadratics): The applications of quadratic equations are the real situations that a squared relationship models: the height of a thrown object over time, the area of a bordered region, a pair of unknown numbers, the price that maximises profit, and speed-distance-time journeys with a current. In every case you translate the words into a quadratic equation in standard form, solve it, and then reject any root that cannot exist physically, such as a negative time or a negative length.
- [Applications of Linear Equations — Real-World Uses](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/applications-linear-equations): An application of a linear equation is any real-world problem you solve by translating words into an equation of the form $ax + b = c$ and solving for the unknown. This article covers the translation method, six worked applications, the mistakes that trip students up, and where these equations show up in daily life.
- [Arithmetic Progressions — nth Term, Sum, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/arithmetic-progressions): An arithmetic progression (AP) is a sequence where each term increases by a fixed amount called the common difference $d$, like $2, 5, 8, 11, \dots$. This article covers the definition, the nth-term formula $a_n = a + (n-1)d$, both sum formulas, a quick derivation, six worked examples, and the mistakes students make most
- [Augmented Matrix: Definition and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/augmented-matrix): An augmented matrix writes a system of linear equations as a single grid $[A \mid b]$, the coefficient matrix $A$, a vertical bar standing in for the equals sign, and the constants $b$. This article shows how to build one from a system, how row operations solve it, and the mistakes that scramble the setup.
- [Basic Facts About Logarithms: Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/basic-facts-about-logarithms): The basic facts about logarithms come down to one idea: a logarithm answers the question "what power do I raise the base to?" So $\log_b x = y$ means exactly $b^y = x$. The base must be positive and not equal to $1$, the argument $x$ must be positive, and three laws (product, quotient, power) turn hard multiplication into easy addition.
- [Basics of Algebra — Variables, Expressions, Equations](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/basic-of-algebra): The basics of algebra are five objects — variables, constants, expressions, equations, and inequalities — combined by the four arithmetic operations under one balance rule. This article walks through each foundational concept with three worked examples, the most common beginner errors, and the 9th-century origin of the word algebra itself.
- [Bijective Function — One-One and Onto, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/bijective-function): A bijective function is one that is both one-one (injective) and onto (surjective) — every output is reached by exactly one input, with no collisions and nothing left out. This article gives the definition, the two conditions, the one-to-one correspondence idea, the $n!$ counting formula, the link to inverses, and six worked examples
- [Binary Operation: Definition, Properties & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/binary-operation): A binary operation on a set $S$ is a rule that takes two elements of $S$ and returns exactly one element that is still in $S$. Addition and multiplication on the integers are binary operations; division on the integers is not, because $6 \div 4$ lands outside the set. The properties worth knowing are closure, commutativity, associativity, the identity element, and the inverse element.
- [Binomial — Definition, Examples, Operations](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/binomial): A binomial is an algebraic expression with exactly two unlike terms — like $3x + 5$ or $x^2 - 9$. This article covers the definition, three worked examples at Quick/Standard/Stretch tiers, the four basic operations on binomials (with the FOIL method for multiplication), and the link to the binomial theorem for higher powers.
- [Cardinality of a Set — Definition, Symbol, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/cardinality): The cardinality of a set is the number of elements it contains, written $|A|$ or $n(A)$ — so the set ${2, 4, 6, 8}$ has cardinality $4$. This article covers the notation, finite versus infinite sets, countable versus uncountable infinity (aleph-null), the power-set rule $|P(A)| = 2^n$, the union formula, and six worked examples.
- [Cayley-Hamilton Theorem: Statement & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/cayley-hamilton-theorem): The Cayley-Hamilton theorem states that every square matrix satisfies its own characteristic equation. You build the characteristic polynomial $p(\lambda) = \det(A - \lambda I)$, then replace the variable $\lambda$ with the matrix $A$ itself, and the result is always the zero matrix: $p(A) = O$. That one fact turns slow jobs, finding an inverse or a high power of a matrix, into short calculations.
- [Coefficient - Definition, Types, and Math Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/coefficient): A coefficient is the number that multiplies a variable in an algebraic expression — in $3x$, the coefficient is $3$. This article covers the formal definition, the four types of coefficients, how to identify them in any expression, the rule for variables with no visible number, and the most common mistakes when picking out the coefficient.
- [Coefficients In Polynomials: What They Do](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/importance-of-coefficients-in-polynomials): Coefficients in polynomials are the numbers multiplying each power of the variable, and they carry almost all of the polynomial's information. The leading coefficient controls the degree and the end behaviour of the graph, the constant term gives the y-intercept, and together the coefficients fix the sum of the roots ($-b/a$), the product of the roots ($c/a$), and, through the discriminant, how many real roots exist.
- [Coinitial Vectors: Definition, Examples & Diagrams](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/coinitial-vectors): Coinitial vectors are two or more vectors that start at the same point, called the initial point or tail, no matter which way they point or how long they are. Sharing a start is the only requirement, so coinitial vectors are easy to mix up with collinear vectors (same line), equal vectors (same magnitude and direction), and coterminous vectors (same endpoint), which is exactly the confusion this guide clears up.
- [Column Matrix - Definition, Order n×1, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/column-matrix): A column matrix is a matrix with exactly one column and any number of rows, so its order is written $n \times 1$. This article covers the definition, the order, the properties, how column matrices add and multiply, the difference between a column and a row matrix, and six worked examples.
- [Common Difference — Arithmetic Progression Definition](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/common-difference): The common difference $d$ in an arithmetic progression is the fixed value added to each term to get the next. $d = a_n - a_{n-1}$, the same for every consecutive pair. This article covers the definition, three worked examples, the $n$th-term and sum formulas that use $d$, and how to find $d$ when only two non-adjacent terms are known.
- [Comparison Test for Series: Convergence & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/comparison-test): The Comparison Test decides whether a positive-term series converges or diverges by comparing it, term by term, to a series you already understand. If $0 \le a_n \le b_n$ and $\sum b_n$ converges, then $\sum a_n$ converges. If $a_n \ge b_n \ge 0$ and $\sum b_n$ diverges, then $\sum a_n$ diverges. The benchmarks you compare against are almost always a p-series or a geometric series.
- [Complement of a Set — Definition, Symbol, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/complement-of-a-set): The complement of a set $A$, written $A'$ (or $A^c$, or $\overline{A}$), is the set of all elements in the universal set $U$ that are not in $A$. This article covers the definition, the symbol, three worked examples, the properties, and the common slips students make when working with complements.
- [Composition of Functions: fog, Domain & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/composition-of-functions): Composition of Functions feeds the output of one function into another: $(f \circ g)(x) = f(g(x))$ means apply $g$ first, then $f$. Order matters, so $f \circ g$ and $g \circ f$ are usually different functions. The domain of a composite is every input allowed by the inner function whose output is also allowed by the outer function, and being able to split a function into a composition is the exact skill the chain rule depends on.
- [Compound Inequality — AND, OR, Solving & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/compound-inequality): A compound inequality joins two inequalities with the word "and" or "or", "and" gives the intersection (values satisfying both), "or" gives the union (values satisfying either). This article shows how to solve and graph each type on a number line, writes the answers in interval notation, and flags where the AND/OR logic most often goes wrong
- [Constant Polynomial - Definition, Degree, and Graph](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/constant-polynomial): A constant polynomial is a polynomial with no variable term, written $p(x) = c$ where $c$ is a fixed non-zero number - its degree is $0$. This article covers the definition, why the degree is zero, how a constant polynomial differs from the zero polynomial, its horizontal-line graph, and six worked examples.
- [Constants in Math: Definition, Examples & Types](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/constants): A constant is a fixed value that does not change, such as the number 5, the fraction $\tfrac{1}{2}$, or a named value like $\pi$. In an expression, the constant term is the part with no variable attached.
- [Constraints On Base Of A Logarithm Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/constraints-on-base): The constraints on base of a logarithm are two short rules: for $\log_b(x)$ the base $b$ must be greater than $0$ and must not equal $1$. On top of that, the argument $x$ must be positive. Each rule traces back to one idea, that a logarithm only makes sense as the exact inverse of an exponential $b^x$, and that inverse falls apart when the base is $0$, $1$, or negative.
- [Continuity of a Function: Definition & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/continuity-of-a-function): Continuity of a function captures the idea of a graph with no breaks, holes, or jumps. A function $f$ is continuous at $x = a$ when three conditions all hold: $f(a)$ is defined, $\lim_{x \to a} f(x)$ exists (the left and right limits agree), and $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = f(a)$. Miss any one of the three and the function is discontinuous at that point.
- [Convergence and Divergence of Series: Tests & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/convergence-and-divergence-of-series): The convergence and divergence of series is decided by one thing: whether the sequence of partial sums $S_N = \sum_{n=1}^{N} a_n$ settles on a finite number as $N \to \infty$. If it does, the series converges to that number; if it does not, the series diverges. Two benchmark families anchor everything: a geometric series $\sum ar^n$ converges exactly when $|r| < 1$, and a p-series $\sum \frac{1}{n^p}$ converges exactly when $p > 1$.
- [Cubic Polynomials — Definition, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/cubic-polynomials): A cubic polynomial is a polynomial of degree 3, of the form $p(x) = ax^3 + bx^2 + cx + d$ with $a \neq 0$. This article covers the definition, the shape and roots of its graph, three worked examples, the most common slips, and the 16th-century rivalry between del Ferro, Tartaglia, and Cardano that produced the cubic formula.
- [Degree Of The Remainder In Polynomial Division](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/degree-of-the-remainder): The degree of the remainder in polynomial division is always strictly less than the degree of the divisor: $\deg(R) < \deg(D)$. So dividing by a linear expression like $x - a$ leaves a constant remainder (degree 0), dividing by a quadratic leaves a remainder of degree at most 1, and the division stops the moment the remainder can no longer be reduced.
- [Degree of a Polynomial: Definition, How to Find, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/degree-of-a-polynomial): The degree of a polynomial is the highest exponent on its variable once like terms are combined. For one variable, read off the largest power; for several variables, add the exponents in each term and take the largest sum. This article covers how to find the degree step by step, the special cases of constant and zero polynomials, the names of polynomials by degree, six worked examples, and the mistakes that cost marks.
- [Derivative of a Function: Definition, Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/derivative-of-a-function): The derivative of a function measures its instantaneous rate of change, and geometrically it is the slope of the tangent line to the graph at a point. It is defined by the limit $f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \dfrac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}$, and in practice you compute it with a small toolbox of rules: power, constant-multiple, sum, product, quotient, and chain. A function that is differentiable at a point is automatically continuous there.
- [Derivatives Of Logarithmic Functions: Rules & Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/derivatives-of-logarithmic-functions): The derivatives of logarithmic functions come down to three formulas: $\frac{d}{dx}\ln x = \frac{1}{x}$, $\frac{d}{dx}\log_a x = \frac{1}{x\ln a}$, and the chain-rule form $\frac{d}{dx}\ln u = \frac{u'}{u}$. Each follows from the derivative of $e^x$ by implicit differentiation, and each reads geometrically as the slope of a logarithm curve, always positive and shrinking as $x$ grows.
- [Derivatives of Exponential Functions: Rules & Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/derivatives-of-exponential-functions): The derivatives of exponential functions come down to two rules: $\frac{d}{dx}e^{x}=e^{x}$, the one function that equals its own derivative, and $\frac{d}{dx}a^{x}=a^{x}\ln a$ for any base $a>0$. Wrap an exponential around an inner function and the chain rule attaches its derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}e^{u}=e^{u},u'$ and $\frac{d}{dx}a^{u}=a^{u}\ln a\cdot u'$. Get the base right, keep the inner derivative, and never reach for the power rule, and every problem in this topic follows the same short recipe.
- [Descartes Rule of Signs - Steps & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/descartes-rule-of-signs): Descartes rule of signs counts the sign changes in a polynomial's coefficients to bound how many positive and negative real roots it can have — the count of positive roots equals the number of sign changes in $f(x)$, or less by an even number. This article gives the steps, a possible-roots chart, the link to complex roots, six worked examples, and the mistakes that throw off the count.
- [Diagonal Matrix - Definition, Properties, Inverse, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/diagonal-matrix): A diagonal matrix is a square matrix with zeros off the main diagonal. Learn its properties, determinant, inverse, special cases, and worked examples.
- [Difference Between Exponent and Power Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/difference-between-exponent-and-power): The difference between exponent and power is one of notation versus expression: the exponent is the small raised number that says how many times to multiply the base, while the power is the whole expression, base and exponent together, and often its computed result. This article defines each term, sets them side by side in a table, shows when the words genuinely differ, and works through examples.
- [Difference Between Permutation and Combination](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/difference-between-permutation-and-combination): Permutation vs Combination is whether order matters: a permutation counts arrangements (order matters), while a combination counts selections (order doesn't). This article covers both definitions, the $nPr$ and $nCr$ formulas derived from scratch, the relationship between them, and how to tell which one a problem needs.
- [Differentiability Of A Function: Definition & Rules](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/differentiability-of-a-function): The differentiability of a function at a point $a$ means the derivative $f'(a) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(a+h) - f(a)}{h}$ exists as a finite number, which requires the left-hand and right-hand difference-quotient limits to agree. Every differentiable function is continuous, but the reverse is false: a function can be continuous yet fail to be differentiable at a corner, a cusp, a vertical tangent, or a break.
- [Discontinuous Function - Types, Graph & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/discontinuous-function): A discontinuous function is one whose graph cannot be drawn without lifting your pen — it has at least one break, hole, jump, or vertical asymptote. This article walks through the three main types — removable, jump, and infinite discontinuities — shows how to read each off a graph, and works through six examples with the mistakes to avoid.
- [Discrete Random Variable - Definition, PMF, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/discrete-random-variable): A discrete random variable is a variable whose value is a number attached to the outcome of a random experiment, and whose possible values you can count - like the number of heads in three coin flips. This article covers the definition, the probability mass function (PMF), how to compute expected value and variance, the common types, and six worked examples.
- [Disjoint Sets: Definition, Venn Diagram & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/disjoint-sets): Disjoint sets are two or more sets that share no common element, so their intersection is the empty set: $A \cap B = \emptyset$. On a Venn diagram they show up as separate circles that never overlap, and because there is nothing shared to subtract, the size of their union is simply $n(A \cup B) = n(A) + n(B)$.
- [Dividing Monomials - Quotient Rule, Steps, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/dividing-monomials): Dividing monomials splits into two independent jobs: divide the numerical coefficients like ordinary numbers, then subtract the exponents of each matching variable using the quotient rule of exponents, $\frac{x^m}{x^n} = x^{m-n}$. This article gives the rule, the special cases (negative exponents, negative coefficients, the zero-exponent result), six worked examples, and the mistakes that cost marks.
- [Dividing Polynomials By Monomials: Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/dividing-polynomials-by-monomials): Dividing polynomials by monomials means splitting the fraction so each term of the polynomial is divided by the monomial on its own. Divide the coefficients as ordinary numbers, then apply the quotient rule for exponents by subtracting the powers of matching variables. Check the result by multiplying it back by the monomial to recover the original polynomial.
- [Dividing Polynomials: Methods, Synthetic Division, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/dividing-polynomials): Dividing polynomials means choosing the right method for the divisor: split terms for a monomial, long division for any larger divisor, and synthetic division as a shortcut when you divide by $x - k$. Every method arranges polynomials in standard form first and inserts zeros for missing powers. This article covers all three methods, how to read the quotient and remainder, when each method applies, and six worked examples from a one-step monomial split to a synthetic division with a remainder.
- [Division Algorithm For General Divisors Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/division-algorithm-for-general-divisors): The division algorithm for general divisors says that for any polynomial $P(x)$ and any non-zero divisor $D(x)$, there is exactly one quotient $Q(x)$ and one remainder $R(x)$ with $P(x) = D(x),Q(x) + R(x)$, where either $R(x) = 0$ or the degree of $R(x)$ is strictly less than the degree of $D(x)$. "General" means the divisor can have any degree, not just degree 1, so you keep dividing until the remainder's degree drops below the divisor's.
- [Division Algorithm For Linear Divisors Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/division-algorithm-for-linear-divisors): The division algorithm for linear divisors says that dividing any polynomial $P(x)$ by a linear divisor $(x - a)$ gives $P(x) = (x - a)Q(x) + r$, where the quotient $Q(x)$ has a degree one lower than $P(x)$ and the remainder $r$ is always a single constant. You can find that quotient and remainder by long division or, much faster, by synthetic division, and the Remainder Theorem tells you the remainder without dividing at all: $r = P(a)$.
- [Division Algorithm for Polynomials - Steps & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/division-algorithm-for-polynomials): The division algorithm for polynomials states that for any polynomials $p(x)$ and $g(x)$ with $g(x) \neq 0$, there exist unique polynomials $q(x)$ and $r(x)$ where $p(x) = g(x)q(x) + r(x)$ and either $r(x) = 0$ or $\deg r(x) < \deg g(x)$. This article covers the statement, what each term means, the division steps, six worked examples, and the mistakes that trip students up.
- [Division Of A Polynomial By A Linear Factor](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/division-of-polynomial-by-linear-factor): Division Of A Polynomial By A Linear Factor means dividing a polynomial $P(x)$ by a first-degree term such as $(x - a)$, which always leaves a polynomial quotient and a single constant remainder. You can do it with long division or the faster synthetic division, and the Remainder Theorem gives the remainder in one step as $P(a)$. When that remainder is zero, $(x - a)$ is a factor of $P(x)$.
- [Division Of Algebraic Expressions: Methods](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/division-of-algebraic-expressions): Division of algebraic expressions means splitting one expression by another using three cases: monomial by monomial (divide coefficients, subtract exponents), polynomial by monomial (divide every term), and polynomial by polynomial (factor and cancel, or use long division). When the division is not exact, a remainder is left, and the result is checked with the identity dividend $=$ divisor $\times$ quotient $+$ remainder.
- [Domain And Range Of A Function: Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/domain-and-range-of-a-function): The domain and range of a function are, respectively, the set of allowable inputs $x$ and the set of resulting outputs $y$. To find a domain you exclude the inputs that break the rule (division by zero, even roots of negatives, logs of non-positive numbers); to find a range you track the outputs algebraically, from the graph, or with calculus using extrema, asymptotes, and end behaviour.
- [Eigenvalues: Definition, How to Find Them & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/eigenvalues): An eigenvalue is the scalar $\lambda$ by which a matrix stretches or shrinks a special vector — its eigenvector — without turning it. This article covers the defining equation $A\mathbf{v} = \lambda\mathbf{v}$, how to find eigenvalues from the characteristic equation $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$, the trace-and-determinant properties, and the sign and factoring mistakes that derail the calculation.
- [Eigenvectors — Definition, Calculation, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/eigenvectors): An eigenvector of a square matrix $A$ is a nonzero vector $v$ that gets stretched (not rotated) by the transformation $A$ — its direction is preserved, only its length is scaled. The scaling factor $\lambda$ is the eigenvalue. This article covers the definition, the two-step calculation, three worked examples, and the appearance of eigenvectors in PageRank, PCA, and quantum mechanics.
- [Elementary Row Operations: Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/elementary-row-operations): Elementary row operations are three legal moves on the rows of a matrix: swapping two rows, scaling a row by a nonzero number, and adding a multiple of one row to another. They power Gaussian elimination, so you use them to reach row echelon form, read off a matrix's rank, solve a system, and build an inverse from an augmented matrix. None of the three changes the solutions of the system the matrix stands for.
- [Elements of a Matrix - Definition, aij Notation, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/elements-of-matrix): The elements of a matrix are its individual entries - each single number, variable, or expression inside the grid. Every element has a unique address, written $a_{ij}$, meaning the entry in row $i$ and column $j$. This article covers the definition, the $a_{ij}$ notation, how to read an element's position, how to count all the elements, diagonal versus off-diagonal elements, and six worked examples.
- [Elimination Method — Steps and Worked Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/elimination-method): The elimination method solves a system of equations by adding or subtracting them so one variable cancels, leaving a single equation in one variable. This article covers the steps, when to add versus subtract, how to scale coefficients first, the no-solution and infinite-solution cases, and the mistakes that cost marks.
- [Empty Set — Definition, Symbol, and Properties](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/empty-set): The empty set is the one set that has no elements at all, written $\varnothing$ or ${}$, with cardinality $0$. This article covers its symbol, why there is only one empty set, why it counts as a subset of every set, how it differs from ${0}$ and from the number zero, and the mistakes that trip students up.
- [Equal Sets: Definition, Symbol & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/equal-sets): Equal sets are sets that hold exactly the same elements, so $A = B$ means every member of $A$ is in $B$ and every member of $B$ is in $A$. Order and repetition never change a set, which is why ${1, 2, 3}$ and ${3, 2, 1}$ are equal. Equal is not the same as equivalent: equal sets share the same elements, while equivalent sets only share the same count of elements.
- [Equal Vectors: Definition, Conditions & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/equal-vector): Two vectors are equal vectors when they share the same magnitude and the same direction, no matter where each one starts. In component form the test is simple: every matching component must be equal, so $a_1\hat{i} + a_2\hat{j} + a_3\hat{k}$ equals $b_1\hat{i} + b_2\hat{j} + b_3\hat{k}$ only when $a_1 = b_1$, $a_2 = b_2$, and $a_3 = b_3$. Position is the one thing that never matters.
- [Equality Of Matrices: Rules And Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/equality-of-matrices): Equality of matrices means two matrices count as the same only when they share the same order and every corresponding element matches. If the dimensions differ, or a single pair of matching entries disagrees, the matrices are not equal. That one rule is what lets you turn a matrix equation into a set of ordinary equations and solve for unknowns.
- [Equation in Math - Definition, Types, and Parts](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/equation): An equation in math is a statement that two expressions are equal, separated by an equals sign — for example, $3x + 5 = 14$. This article covers the formal definition, every part of an equation, the seven main types you'll meet in school, and the most common mistakes when solving them.
- [Equivalence Relations: Definition, Rules & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/equivalence-relations): An equivalence relation is a rule that links two things when they share one specific feature, and it must pass three tests at once: reflexive, symmetric, and transitive. Equality, congruence modulo n, and "same remainder" all qualify, while strict inequality and perpendicularity do not. Every equivalence relation splits a set into non-overlapping groups called equivalence classes, and those classes form a partition.
- [Even and Odd Functions — Definition, Tests, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/even-and-odd-function): A function $f$ is even when $f(-x) = f(x)$ (its graph is symmetric about the $y$-axis) and odd when $f(-x) = -f(x)$ (its graph is symmetric about the origin). This article covers the algebraic test, three worked examples at Quick/Standard/Stretch tiers, the addition and multiplication rules, and what happens when a function is neither.
- [Exponential Form — Converting from Logarithmic & Radical](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/exponential-form): Exponential form writes repeated multiplication as a base raised to an exponent — for example $8 = 2^3$ instead of $2 \times 2 \times 2$. This article covers what exponential form is, how to convert to and from logarithmic and radical forms, the conversion-pattern table that captures every move on one page, three worked examples, and the mistakes that quietly cost marks.
- [Exponential Functions: Derivatives And Integrals](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/exponential-functions): Exponential functions have the form $f(x) = a^x$ with $a > 0$ and $a \neq 1$, and the star of calculus is the natural exponential $f(x) = e^x$, the one function that is its own derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x$. For a general base, $\frac{d}{dx}(a^x) = a^x \ln a$ and $\int a^x , dx = \frac{a^x}{\ln a} + C$, while the growth-and-decay model $y = y_0 e^{kt}$ describes anything whose rate of change is proportional to its current size.
- [Exponents — Definition, Laws, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/exponents): An exponent tells you how many times to multiply a number, the base, by itself — so $2^5$ means $2 \times 2 \times 2 \times 2 \times 2 = 32$. This article covers what an exponent is, the parts of a power, the seven laws of exponents at a glance, negative, fractional, zero, and decimal exponents, where exponents show up in the real world, and the slips that cost marks.
- [Expression in Math - Definition, Types, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/expression-definition): A mathematical expression is a combination of numbers, variables, and operations that represents a value — but contains no equals sign. Examples: $3x + 5$, $2y^2 - 7$, $\sqrt{x + 1}$. An expression with an equals sign attached becomes an equation.
- [Expression, Term, Factor, Coefficient — Definitions, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/expression-term-factor-and-coefficient): Every algebraic expression is built from terms (parts joined by + or −), factors (parts multiplied inside a term), and coefficients (the number multiplying the variable). This article defines all four, shows exactly how to spot each one, covers like versus unlike terms, and works through examples that take an expression apart piece by piece.
- [Factor Theorem - Statement, Proof, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factor-theorem): The factor theorem states that for a polynomial $p(x)$, the linear expression $(x - a)$ is a factor if and only if $p(a) = 0$. This article gives the statement, both directions of the proof, how to use it to factor polynomials, six worked examples, and the common mistakes to avoid.
- [Factored Form - Definition, Conversion, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factored-form): The factored form of a quadratic writes it as a product of linear factors — for example $x^2 + 5x + 6$ becomes $(x+2)(x+3)$. This article covers what factored form is, how it compares to standard form and vertex form, how to convert between them, three worked examples at increasing difficulty, and the mistakes that quietly cost marks.
- [Factoring Cubic Polynomials — Methods & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factoring-cubic-polynomials): To factor a cubic polynomial, three reliable methods cover almost every case — the Rational Root Theorem, the grouping method, and the sum/difference of cubes identities. This article walks through all three with three worked examples spanning Quick to Stretch, the mistakes that cost marks, and the people who built the framework.
- [Factoring Methods — Definition, Types, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factoring-methods): Factoring methods are the standard techniques for rewriting a polynomial as a product of simpler factors: pulling out the greatest common factor (GCF), factoring by grouping, factoring trinomials, and the difference of squares pattern $a^2 - b^2 = (a+b)(a-b)$.
- [Factoring Polynomials: Methods & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factoring-polynomials): Factoring polynomials means rewriting a polynomial as a product of simpler polynomials, the reverse of expanding brackets. The reliable order is to pull out the greatest common factor first, then match the leftover to a pattern: difference of squares, perfect-square trinomial, sum or difference of cubes, grouping for four terms, or splitting the middle term for a trinomial. For degree three and higher, the factor theorem plus synthetic division finds the factors.
- [Factoring Trinomials — Methods, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factoring-trinomials): Factoring trinomials rewrites $ax^2 + bx + c$ as a product of two binomials by finding two numbers whose product is $ac$ and whose sum is $b$. This article covers the three standard methods (sum-product when $a = 1$, AC/grouping when $a \neq 1$, and the perfect-square shortcut) with three worked examples and the most common slip students make.
- [Factorization Of Quadratic Equations: Steps](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factorization-of-quadratic-equations): The factorization of quadratic equations means rewriting $ax^2 + bx + c = 0$ as a product of two linear factors, then setting each factor to zero to read off the roots. The core method is splitting the middle term: find two numbers whose product is $a \cdot c$ and whose sum is $b$. When no such rational pair exists, the roots are still there, but you switch to the quadratic formula.
- [Factorization of Algebraic Expressions - Methods and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factorization-of-algebraic-expressions): Factorization of algebraic expressions means rewriting an expression as a product of its factors — turning a sum like $x^2 + 5x + 6$ into $(x+2)(x+3)$. This article walks through the four core methods (common factor, grouping, algebraic identities, and splitting the middle term), worked examples, and the errors that trip students up
- [Factorization of Quadratic Polynomials - Methods](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factorization-of-quadratic-polynomials): Factorizing a quadratic polynomial $ax^2 + bx + c$ means rewriting it as a product of two linear factors, usually by splitting the middle term so the two pieces multiply to $ac$ and add to $b$. This article covers four methods, six worked examples, and the sign errors that derail most attempts.
- [Factors Of A Polynomial: Methods & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/factors-of-a-polynomial): Factors of a polynomial are the smaller polynomials that multiply together to build it, each dividing it with zero remainder. The fastest way to find them is the factor theorem: $(x - a)$ is a factor exactly when $P(a) = 0$. From there you use the greatest common factor, grouping, algebraic identities, and synthetic division to break the polynomial down completely.
- [Finite And Infinite Sets: Definition & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/finite-and-infinite-sets): A finite set has a countable number of elements, so its cardinality $n(A)$ is a whole number, while an infinite set has endlessly many, so no whole number can count it. The real split between finite and infinite sets is not how big a set looks but whether the counting ever stops. And a strange twist waits at the end: not all infinite sets are the same size.
- [Floor and Ceiling Function — Definition and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/floor-and-ceiling-function): The floor function $\lfloor x \rfloor$ gives the greatest integer less than or equal to $x$, and the ceiling function $\lceil x \rceil$ gives the least integer greater than or equal to $x$. This article covers their notation, graphs, properties, and worked examples, plus the negative-number trap that costs the most marks.
- [Fractional Exponents — Rules, Examples & How to Simplify](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/fractional-exponents): A fractional exponent is a root written as a power — $a^{1/n}$ means the $n$-th root of $a$, and $a^{m/n}$ means take the $n$-th root and raise it to the $m$-th power. This article covers the rules that govern fractional exponents, three worked examples at increasing difficulty, the three mistakes that quietly cost marks, and where rational powers turn up beyond the algebra classroom.
- [Graphically Solving A Pair Of Linear Equations](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/graphically-solving-a-pair-of-linear-equations): Graphically solving a pair of linear equations means drawing both equations as straight lines on the same grid and reading off where they cross. That intersection point is the one $(x, y)$ that makes both equations true at once. Two lines can cross once (one solution), run parallel and never meet (no solution), or lie exactly on top of each other (infinitely many solutions).
- [Graphing Functions: A Calculus Step-By-Step Guide](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/graphing-functions): Graphing functions with calculus means building an accurate curve from what the equation tells you, in seven steps: domain, intercepts, symmetry, asymptotes, the first derivative $f'(x)$ (where the curve rises or falls and where its peaks and valleys sit), the second derivative $f''(x)$ (where it bends up or down and where it changes bend), then the assembled sketch. The first derivative gives the slope; the second derivative gives the concavity. Together they let you draw a curve you have never plotted, without guessing at points.
- [Graphing Linear Equations: Methods, Steps, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/graphing-linear-equations): Graphing linear equations means plotting the straight line that satisfies an equation like $y = mx + b$. This article walks through the three main methods — slope-intercept, table of values, and intercepts — with worked examples and the mistakes that produce a crooked or misplaced line.
- [Graphs Of Quadratic Expressions Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/graphs-of-quadratic-expressions-theory): Graphs of quadratic expressions are always parabolas, the U-shaped curve of $y = ax^2 + bx + c$. The sign of $a$ decides whether the curve opens up or down, the vertex sits at $x = -\dfrac{b}{2a}$ with the axis of symmetry running through it, $c$ is the y-intercept, and the discriminant $b^2 - 4ac$ tells you whether the curve meets the x-axis at two points, one point, or none.
- [Greatest Integer Function — Graph, Domain, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/greatest-integer-function): The greatest integer function, written $\lfloor x \rfloor$, returns the largest integer that is less than or equal to $x$ — so it rounds every number down to a whole number. This article covers its definition, the staircase graph, domain and range, key properties, the rule for negative numbers, and six worked examples.
- [Hermitian Matrix - Definition, Properties, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/hermitian-matrix): A Hermitian matrix is a square complex matrix that equals its own conjugate transpose, written $A = A^{H}$. This article covers the definition, what the conjugate transpose is, why the diagonal entries and eigenvalues are always real, how a Hermitian matrix differs from a symmetric one, and six worked examples.
- [Homogeneous Function: Definition, Degree & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/homogeneous-function): A homogeneous function of degree $n$ is one where scaling every input by the same factor $t$ scales the output by $t^n$, written $f(tx, ty) = t^n f(x, y)$.
