- [Cylindrical Coordinates - Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/cylindrical-coordinates): Cylindrical coordinates locate a point in 3D space as the ordered triple (r, θ, z), where $(r, \theta)$ are the polar coordinates of the point's shadow on the xy-plane and z is the usual height. This article gives the conversion formulas to and from Cartesian coordinates, shows when the system is worth using, and works through six examples plus the mistakes to avoid.
- [Cylindrical Shell Method: Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/cylindrical-shell-method): The cylindrical shell method finds the volume of a solid of revolution by adding up thin nested tubes instead of flat slices. For a region revolved about the $y$-axis, the volume is $V = \int_a^b 2\pi,(\text{radius})(\text{height}),dx = \int_a^b 2\pi x,f(x),dx$. Its advantage over the disk and washer methods is that you integrate along the axis perpendicular to the axis of revolution, so you rarely have to solve the curve for its inverse.
- [Decagon - Definition, Sides, Angles, Diagonals, and Area Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/decagon): A decagon is a ten-sided polygon whose interior angles sum to $1440°$; in a regular decagon each interior angle is $144°$ and each exterior angle is $36°$. This article defines the decagon, derives its area formula $A = \tfrac{5}{2}a^2\sqrt{5+2\sqrt{5}}$, counts its 35 diagonals, and works through examples, starting with the ten-sided coin in circulation today
- [Definition Of The Derivative: Limit Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/definition-of-the-derivative): The definition of the derivative is $f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \dfrac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}$: it measures the instantaneous rate of change of $f$ at $x$, which equals the slope of the tangent line to the graph. You get it by shrinking a secant line between two nearby points until the two points merge, so the average rate of change turns into an instantaneous one. Every differentiation rule you will ever use is built from this single limit.
- [Degrees to Radians: Formula, Conversion Steps, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/converting-degrees-to-radians): To convert degrees to radians, multiply the degree measure by $\frac{\pi}{180}$, because 180° equals π radians. This article gives the formula $\text{radians} = \text{degrees} \times \frac{\pi}{180}$, explains why the factor is $\frac{\pi}{180}$, provides a common-angle conversion table, worked examples, and shows how this differs from the reverse conversion.
- [Derivative Formula: Rules, List & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/derivative): The **derivative formula**, $f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \dfrac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}$, measures a function's instantaneous rate of change at a single point, applied through standard rules — power, sum, product, quotient, and chain — together with the derivatives of trig, exponential, logarithmic, and inverse trig functions, with the chain rule and the product rule causing most student errors.
- [Derivative of Arccos x — Formula, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/derivative-of-arccos): The derivative of arccos $x$ is $\dfrac{d}{dx}\arccos x = -\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}$ on the open interval $(-1, 1)$ — a negative quantity that reflects the fact arccosine is a strictly decreasing function. This article gives the implicit-differentiation derivation, the first-principles approach, the chain-rule version $\dfrac{d}{dx}\arccos(u) = -\dfrac{u'}{\sqrt{1-u^2}}$, three worked examples, and the common mistakes around sign and domain.
- [Derivative of Cos 2x : Formula, Chain Rule & Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/derivative-of-cos2x): The derivative of $\cos 2x$ is $-2\sin 2x$. The extra factor of $2$ comes from the chain rule, because $2x$ is an inner function whose own derivative is $2$. This article covers the formula, the chain-rule proof, a first-principle derivation, the anti-derivative, worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Derivative of Cos Inverse : Formula, Proof & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/derivative-of-cos-inverse): The derivative of $\cos^{-1}x$ (arccos x) is $\dfrac{d}{dx}\cos^{-1}x = -\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^{2}}}$, valid for $-1 < x < 1$. This article covers the formula, the implicit-differentiation proof, why the sign is negative, the role of the principal branch, worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Derivative of Cosec x : Formula, Proof & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/derivative-of-cosec-x): The derivative of cosec x is $\dfrac{d}{dx}(\csc x) = -\csc x \cot x$. This article proves that result three ways - the quotient rule on $\frac{1}{\sin x}$, the chain rule on $(\sin x)^{-1}$, and the first principle - then works six examples and clears up where the sign and the chain rule go wrong.
- [Derivative of Tan 2x - Formula, Proof, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/derivative-of-tan2x): The derivative of tan 2x is $\dfrac{d}{dx}(\tan 2x) = 2\sec^2 2x$. This article proves that result three ways — chain rule, first principle, and quotient rule — works through examples, and clears up the most common confusion: $\tan 2x$ (double angle) versus $\tan^2 x$ (tangent squared), which have different derivatives.
- [Derivative of Tan Inverse x : Formula, Proof & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/derivative-of-tan-inverse-x): The derivative of tan inverse x is $\dfrac{d}{dx}\big(\tan^{-1}x\big) = \dfrac{1}{1+x^2}$. This article derives that result by implicit differentiation and by the first principle, works six examples from bare $\tan^{-1}x$ to composite arguments, and clears up the chain-rule and inverse-versus-reciprocal mistakes that trip students up.
- [Determinant of a Matrix - Formula and Calculation](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/determinant-of-matrix): The determinant of a square matrix is a single number that captures the matrix's area-scaling factor (or volume-scaling in 3D), its invertibility (non-zero $\Leftrightarrow$ invertible), and the orientation it produces (positive or negative). For $2\times 2$: $\det = ad - bc$. For $3 \times 3$: cofactor expansion.
- [Determinants — Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/determinants): A determinant is a single number computed from a square matrix that tells you whether the matrix can be inverted and how it scales area or volume. This article defines determinants, works the 2×2 and 3×3 expansions step by step, lists the properties that speed the arithmetic up, and shows where the answer matters.
- [Diagonal of Cuboid: Formula, Derivation & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/diagonal-of-cuboid): The diagonal of a cuboid (its body diagonal) is the longest straight line inside the solid, found with $d = \sqrt{l^2 + b^2 + h^2}$. This article shows where that formula comes from, how it differs from a face diagonal, six worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Diagonal of Parallelogram Formula — p = √(x² + y² − 2xy·cos A)](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/diagonal-of-parallelogram): The diagonal of parallelogram formula computes a diagonal's length from the two adjacent sides and the included angle: $p = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 - 2xy \cos A}$. This article gives the formula, derives it from the law of cosines, gives the companion parallelogram law $p^2 + q^2 = 2(x^2 + y^2)$, and walks through three worked examples and the most common student mistake.
- [Diagonal of Rectangle - Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/diagonals-of-rectangle): The diagonal of a rectangle is found with $d = \sqrt{l^2 + w^2}$, where $l$ is the length and $w$ is the width - it is the hypotenuse of the right triangle each diagonal creates. This guide derives the formula, works six examples, and clears up the mistakes that give wrong diagonal lengths.
- [Diagonal of Rhombus — Formula, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/diagonal-of-rhombus): The two diagonals of a rhombus bisect each other at right angles, splitting it into four congruent right triangles. That single fact gives you every diagonal formula: from area, $p = \dfrac{2 \times \text{Area}}{q}$, and from the side, $\left(\dfrac{p}{2}\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{q}{2}\right)^2 = a^2$. This guide derives both and works through six examples.
- [Diagonal of Square Formula — d = s√2](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/diagonal-of-square): The diagonal of square formula is $d = s\sqrt{2}$, where $s$ is the side length — the diagonal of any square is always its side times the square root of 2. This article derives the formula from the Pythagorean theorem, shows how to find the diagonal from the side, the area, or the perimeter, works six examples from a one-step computation to a real-world problem, and clears up the mistakes that cost the most marks.
- [Diameter Formula — d = 2r, d = C/π, d = 2√(A/π)](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/diameter-formula): The diameter formula has three forms depending on what you already know: $d = 2r$ from the radius, $d = \frac{C}{\pi}$ from the circumference, and $d = 2\sqrt{\frac{A}{\pi}}$ from the area. This article gives all three, shows where each comes from, works six examples from a one-step doubling to a real-world measurement problem, and clears up the radius-versus-diameter mix-ups that trip most students.
- [Diameter of a Circle — Formula, Worked Examples, and Properties](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/diameter-of-a-circle): The diameter of a circle is the longest chord, passing through the centre, equal to twice the radius. Formula: $d = 2r$, or $d = C/\pi$ from circumference, or $d = 2\sqrt{A/\pi}$ from area. This article gives the three diameter formulas, three worked examples (Quick, Standard, Stretch), and the historical thread from Archimedes to modern usage.
- [Difference Quotient: Formula, Examples & Meaning](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/difference-quotient): The difference quotient is the expression $\dfrac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}$, and it measures the average rate of change of a function $f$ over the interval from $x$ to $x+h$. Geometrically it is the slope of the secant line joining two points on the curve. Letting the step shrink, $\lim_{h \to 0} \dfrac{f(x+h)-f(x)}{h}$, turns that average slope into the derivative $f'(x)$, the instantaneous rate of change and the slope of the tangent line.
- [Difference of Sets: Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/difference-of-sets): The difference of sets $A - B$ is the set of elements that belong to $A$ but not to $B$: $A - B = {x : x \in A \text{ and } x \notin B}$. This article shows the formula, the Venn diagram, worked examples, and why $A - B \neq B - A$.
- [Differentiation of Trigonometric Functions — Formulas & Rules](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/differentiation-of-trigonometric-functions): The differentiation of trigonometric functions gives the six core rules: $\frac{d}{dx}\sin x = \cos x$, $\frac{d}{dx}\cos x = -\sin x$, $\frac{d}{dx}\tan x = \sec^2 x$, $\frac{d}{dx}\cot x = -\csc^2 x$, $\frac{d}{dx}\sec x = \sec x \tan x$, and $\frac{d}{dx}\csc x = -\csc x \cot x$. All six follow from the sine and cosine derivatives via the quotient rule. This article proves them from first principles and shows where students slip.
- [Direction Cosine - Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/direction-cosine): The direction cosines of a vector are the cosines of the three angles $\alpha, \beta, \gamma$ it makes with the positive $x$, $y$, and $z$ axes, written $l = \cos\alpha$, $m = \cos\beta$, $n = \cos\gamma$. For a vector $a,\hat{i} + b,\hat{j} + c,\hat{k}$ they are each component over the magnitude, and they always satisfy $l^2 + m^2 + n^2 = 1$. This guide derives the formulas, proves that identity, and works six examples.
- [Direction Ratio : Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/direction-ratio): A direction ratio of a line or vector is any set of three numbers (a, b, c) proportional to its components along the x, y, and z axes. This article defines direction ratios, shows how they connect to direction cosines through $l^2 + m^2 + n^2 = 1$, and works six examples from finding ratios to computing the angle between two lines.
- [Directrix of Ellipse - Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/directrix-of-ellipse): The directrix of an ellipse is a fixed straight line, perpendicular to the major axis, given by $x = \pm\dfrac{a}{e}$ for the ellipse $\dfrac{x^2}{a^2} + \dfrac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$, where $e$ is the eccentricity. An ellipse has two directrices, one for each focus, and together with the focus they define the ellipse through the ratio $e < 1$. This guide derives the formula and works six examples.
- [Discriminant in Quadratic Equations - Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/discriminant): The discriminant $\Delta = b^2 - 4ac$ is the piece under the square root in the quadratic formula. Its sign tells you the nature of a quadratic's roots before you finish solving — two distinct real roots ($\Delta > 0$), one repeated real root ($\Delta = 0$), or two complex roots ($\Delta < 0$).
- [Distance Between Point and Plane: Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/distance-between-point-and-plane): The distance between a point $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ and a plane $ax + by + cz + d = 0$ is the shortest, perpendicular distance, given by $\dfrac{|ax_0 + by_0 + cz_0 + d|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}}$. This article derives the formula, explains what every term does, works six examples, and shows that a distance of zero means the point lies on the plane.
- [Distance Between Two Lines: Formula and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/distance-between-two-lines): The distance between two lines is the length of the shortest segment joining them, measured perpendicular to both. For parallel lines $ax + by + c_1 = 0$ and $ax + by + c_2 = 0$ it is $d = \dfrac{|c_2 - c_1|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}$; for skew lines in 3D it uses the cross product. This article defines both cases and works through examples.
- [Distance Between Two Parallel Lines: Formula, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/distance-between-two-parallel-lines): A direction ratio of a line or vector is any set of three numbers (a, b, c) proportional to its components along the x, y, and z axes. This article defines direction ratios, shows how they connect to direction cosines through $l^2 + m^2 + n^2 = 1$, and works six examples from finding ratios to computing the angle between two lines.
- [Distance Between Two Planes: Formula and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/distance-between-two-planes): The distance between two parallel planes $ax + by + cz + d_1 = 0$ and $ax + by + cz + d_2 = 0$ is $\dfrac{|d_1 - d_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}}$, provided their normal coefficients match. This article shows why the planes must be parallel for a distance to exist, why non-parallel planes have distance zero, and works through six examples
- [Distance Between Two Points: Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/distance-between-two-points): The distance between two points $(x_1, y_1)$ and $(x_2, y_2)$ is $\sqrt{(x_2-x_1)^2 + (y_2-y_1)^2}$. This article derives that formula from the Pythagorean theorem, extends it to 3D, and works through six examples so you can measure any straight-line gap on a coordinate plane.
- [Distance Formula in Maths: Definition, Derivation, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/distance-formula): The distance formula, d = √[(x₂ − x₁)² + (y₂ − y₁)²], gives the straight-line distance between two points on a coordinate plane. It is derived from the Pythagorean theorem and extends to 3D by adding a z-coordinate term.
- [Distance of a Point From a Line - Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/distance-of-a-point-from-a-line): The distance of a point from a line is the shortest length between them, measured along the perpendicular dropped from the point to the line. For a point $(x_0, y_0)$ and a line $Ax + By + C = 0$, that distance is $d = \dfrac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}$; this article derives the formula and works six examples.
- [Dividend Divisor Quotient Remainder Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/dividend-divisor-quotient-remainder): The dividend divisor quotient remainder formula — known formally as the Division Algorithm — says $\text{Dividend} = \text{Divisor} \times \text{Quotient} + \text{Remainder}$, with $0 \leq \text{Remainder} < \text{Divisor}$. This article gives the formula, the four reverse formulas (find each term given the other three), three worked examples spanning Quick to Stretch, and the most common slips students make on division-with-remainder problems.
- [Division Property of Equality — Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/division-property-of-equality): The division property of equality states that dividing both sides of a true equation by the same non-zero number keeps it true, so $5x = 25$ becomes $x = 5$. This article gives the formula, six worked examples, why the "non-zero" rule exists, and the errors students make most.
- [Dodecahedron - Definition, Formulas, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/dodecahedron): A dodecahedron is a three-dimensional solid with 12 flat faces; the regular dodecahedron, one of the five Platonic solids, has 12 identical regular pentagon faces, 20 vertices, and 30 edges. This article gives its properties, its surface area $3\sqrt{25 + 10\sqrt{5}},a^2$ and volume $\frac{15 + 7\sqrt{5}}{4},a^3$ formulas, and six worked examples.
- [Dot Product — Formula, Projection, Angle, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/dot-product): The dot product of two vectors multiplies them into a single number, defined two equivalent ways: $\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b} = |\vec{a}||\vec{b}|\cos\theta$ (geometric) and $\vec{a}\cdot\vec{b} = a_1 b_1 + a_2 b_2 + a_3 b_3$ (component). This article covers both formulas, the projection of one vector onto another, the angle between two vectors, every key property, and six worked examples.
- [Double Angle Formulas: Sin, Cos, Tan Identities](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/double-angle): The double angle formulas are sin 2θ = 2 sin θ cos θ, cos 2θ = cos²θ − sin²θ (also 1 − 2 sin²θ and 2 cos²θ − 1), and tan 2θ = 2 tan θ / (1 − tan²θ). All three are derived from the sum formulas by setting α = β = θ. The cosine has three equivalent forms because the Pythagorean identity converts between them.
- [Eccentricity - Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/eccentricity): Eccentricity (e) is a single number that tells you the shape of a conic section: e = 0 for a circle, 0 < e < 1** for an ellipse, **e = 1** for a parabola, and **e > 1 for a hyperbola. This article gives the formula for each conic, worked examples, where eccentricity matters, and the common mistakes.
- [Eccentricity of Hyperbola - Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/eccentricity-of-hyperbola): The eccentricity of a hyperbola measures how "open" the curve is, and it is always greater than 1. For the standard hyperbola $\dfrac{x^2}{a^2} - \dfrac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$, it is given by $e = \dfrac{c}{a} = \sqrt{1 + \dfrac{b^2}{a^2}}$, where $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$. This guide derives the formula, contrasts it with the ellipse, and works six examples.
- [Eccentricity of an Ellipse: Formula, Meaning, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/eccentricity-of-ellipse): The eccentricity of an ellipse is a single number, $e = \dfrac{c}{a}$, that measures how stretched the ellipse is, from a perfect circle to a long, thin oval. This article covers what eccentricity measures, the two equivalent formulas $e = \dfrac{c}{a}$ and $e = \sqrt{1 - \dfrac{b^2}{a^2}}$, why it always lies between 0 and 1, the derivation, and worked examples.
- [Elasticity Of Demand: Formula, Calculus & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/elasticity-of-demand): Elasticity of demand measures how sharply the quantity people buy responds to a change in price, and the calculus version is the point formula $E = \frac{p}{q}\cdot\frac{dq}{dp}$. Classify by size: $|E|>1$ is elastic, $|E|<1$ is inelastic, and $|E|=1$ is unit elastic. Because revenue is $R = pq$, the derivative $\frac{dR}{dp}$ vanishes exactly at unit elasticity, so revenue is maximized where $E = -1$.
- [Ellipse - Equation, Formula, Properties, Graphing](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/ellipse): An ellipse is the set of all points in a plane whose distances to two fixed points (called foci) sum to a constant. Its standard equation is $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ where $a$ is the semi-major axis and $b$ is the semi-minor axis.
- [Equation of a Line - All Forms, Formulas, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/equation-of-a-line): The equation of a line is an algebraic rule every point on a straight line obeys, and it can be written in six standard forms: slope-intercept $y = mx + b$, point-slope, two-point, intercept, normal, and general $Ax + By + C = 0$. This article shows each formula, when to use it, and how to convert between them.
- [Equidistant in Geometry — Meaning, Formula, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/equidistant): Equidistant means being at an equal distance from two or more points, lines, or objects. This article gives the plain meaning, the distance and midpoint formulas that test for it, the role of the perpendicular bisector (every point on it is equidistant from a segment's endpoints), and worked examples — plus where equidistance hides inside circles and triangle centers.
- [Equilateral Triangle: Definition, Properties, and Formulas](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/equilateral-triangle): An equilateral triangle is a triangle with all three sides equal and all three angles equal to $60°$. This article covers its definition, properties, the area $\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}a^2\right)$, perimeter $(3a)$ and height $\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}a\right)$ formulas with derivations, six worked examples, and common mistakes.
- [Euclidean Distance Formula — 2D, 3D & nD](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/euclidean-distance-formula): The euclidean distance formula measures the straight-line distance between two points: in 2D it is $d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}$, a direct consequence of the Pythagorean theorem. This article derives it from a right triangle, extends it to 3D and any number of dimensions, works through examples, and flags the mistakes that cost marks.
- [Explicit Formulas for Sequences - Methods, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/explicit-formulas): An explicit formula for a sequence gives the $n$-th term $a_n$ directly as a function of $n$ — no need to compute the previous terms first. For an arithmetic sequence, the formula is $a_n = a_1 + (n-1)d$. For geometric, $a_n = a_1 \cdot r^{n-1}$. This article covers the derivation, the four most-tested sequence types, worked examples, the three off-by-one mistakes that cost marks, and the mathematicians who built the formulas.
- [Explicit Function — Definition, Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/explicit-function): An explicit function is one where the dependent variable is solved for and written by itself, in the form $y = f(x)$, so you can compute the output straight from the input. This article gives the definition, contrasts explicit with implicit form, works six examples, and names the mistakes students make when converting between the two.
- [Exponent Rules - Laws of Exponents, Formulas, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/exponent-rules): The eight core exponent rules simplify expressions involving powers: the product rule, the quotient rule, the power rule, and supporting rules for the power of a product, power of a quotient, zero exponent, negative exponent, and fractional exponent. This article gives the laws with worked examples, the historical origin, the entities involved, and the most common student mistakes.
- [Exponential Equations — Solving Methods, Formulas & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/exponential-equations): An exponential equation is an equation where the variable sits in the exponent — like $2^x = 32$ or $3^{2x+1} = 81$. This article covers what makes an equation exponential, the three methods used to solve them (same base, logarithms, substitution), three worked examples at increasing difficulty, and the mistakes that quietly cost marks.
- [Exterior Angles of Triangle: Theorem, Formula, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/exterior-angle-of-triangle): An exterior angle of a triangle is formed by extending one side, and it equals the sum of the two non-adjacent (remote) interior angles. Each exterior angle is also supplementary to its adjacent interior angle, and the three exterior angles taken one per vertex sum to 360°. This article covers the exterior angle theorem, the formulas, a short proof, and six worked examples.
- [Exterior Angles of a Polygon: Sum & Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/exterior-angles-of-a-polygon): The exterior angles of a polygon are the angles between each side and the extension of its neighbour, and they always sum to $360°$ — for any polygon, regular or irregular, no matter how many sides. In a regular polygon, each exterior angle is $\dfrac{360°}{n}$. This article defines the exterior angle, derives the $360°$ sum, gives the formula, and works through examples.
- [External Division: Formula & Sign Convention](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/external-division): External division places a point on the extension of a segment, dividing it in the ratio $m:n$ with coordinates $\left(\frac{mx_2-nx_1}{m-n}, \frac{my_2-ny_1}{m-n}\right)$. This article explains the minus-sign convention that sets it apart from internal division, derives the formula, and works six examples, with the section formula as its parent.
- [Fibonacci Numbers — Golden Ratio and Binet's Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/fibonacci-numbers): The Fibonacci numbers are the sequence $0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, \ldots$, where each term is the sum of the two before it: $F_n = F_{n-1} + F_{n-2}$. This article covers the recurrence, the full list, the golden-ratio connection, Binet's formula for the $n$th term, key properties, and six worked examples.
- [Find Side c of a Triangle: Formula, Steps & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/triangle-calc-c): To find side c of a triangle, use Pythagoras ($c^2 = a^2 + b^2$) when the triangle has a right angle, and the Law of Cosines ($c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab\cos C$) when you know two sides and the angle between them. This guide walks through both methods, six worked examples, the triangle-inequality check, and the sign and labelling traps that flip the answer.
- [Finding Slope From Two Points — Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/finding-slope-from-two-points): To find the slope from two points $(x_1, y_1)$ and $(x_2, y_2)$, use the formula $$m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}$$ — the change in y over the change in x, read as "rise over run." This article covers the formula, where it comes from, a clean step-by-step method, six worked examples, and the order-of-subtraction mistake that trips up most students.
- [Foci of Hyperbola — Formula, How to Find, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/foci-of-hyperbola): The foci of a hyperbola are two fixed points whose difference of distances to any point on the curve is constant. They are found from $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$, sitting at $(\pm c, 0)$ for a horizontal hyperbola centered at the origin. This article covers the focus formula, the defining difference-of-distances property, eccentricity, and worked examples on the foci of a hyperbola — note this differs from the ellipse, where $c^2 = a^2 - b^2$.
- [Foci of an Ellipse: Definition, Formula, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/foci-of-ellipse): The foci of an ellipse are two fixed points on its major axis such that, for any point on the ellipse, the sum of its distances to the two foci is constant (equal to 2a). This article covers the definition, where the foci sit, the formula c² = a² − b², six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Focus of a Parabola: Definition, Formula, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/focus-of-parabola): The focus of a parabola is the single fixed point on the axis such that every point on the curve is the same distance from the focus as it is from the directrix line. This article covers the definition, the focus formula for all four standard forms ($y^2 = 4ax$ gives focus $(a, 0)$), the focal distance, the latus rectum and focal chord, six worked examples, and the common mistakes.
- [Foot to Meters Formula — Convert ft to m (×0.3048)](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/foot-to-meters): The foot to meters formula multiplies the length in feet by exactly 0.3048 to give the length in metres. This article gives the formula, the 1959 international yard-and-pound agreement that fixed the conversion factor, three worked examples, the reverse direction (metres → feet), and the common mistake of using 0.3 as a shortcut.
- [Frequency Formula: Definition, Units, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/frequency): *Frequency** is the number of complete cycles that happen in one unit of time. **The formula:** $f = \dfrac{1}{T}$, where $T$ is the time for one cycle (the period). **SI unit:** hertz (Hz). One hertz = one cycle per second.
- [Frustum of a Cone: Definition, Formulas, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/frustum-of-cone): A frustum of a cone is the solid that remains when the top of a cone is cut off by a plane parallel to its base, leaving two circular faces of radii $R$ and $r$. Its volume is $\frac{1}{3}\pi h(R^2 + Rr + r^2)$. This article covers the volume, surface-area, and slant-height formulas, with six worked examples.
- [Geometric Progressions — Formula, Sum, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/geometric-progressions): A geometric progression is a sequence in which every term after the first is found by multiplying the previous term by a fixed non-zero number called the common ratio. This article covers the $n$th-term and sum formulas, three worked examples at Quick/Standard/Stretch tiers, the infinite-sum case, and the 1859 Australian rabbit explosion that turned a geometric progression into a national crisis.
- [Geometric Sequence Formulas: nth Term, Sum, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/geometric-sequence): The geometric sequence formulas cover three things: the nth term aₙ = a · r^(n−1), the sum of the first n terms Sₙ = a(1 − rⁿ)/(1 − r) for r ≠ 1, and the infinite sum S∞ = a/(1 − r) when |r| < 1. The first formula finds any single term; the other two add up multiple terms.
- [Geometric Sequence — Formula, Sum, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/geometric-sequence): A geometric sequence is a list of numbers where every term after the first is found by multiplying the previous term by a fixed common ratio $r$. This article covers the $n$th-term and sum formulas, three worked examples at Quick/Standard/Stretch tiers, the role of $|r| < 1$ for convergent infinite series, and the difference between a sequence and a series.
- [Geometric Series: Convergence, Sum & Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/geometric-series): A geometric series adds the terms of a geometric sequence: $a + ar + ar^2 + ar^3 + \cdots$, where each term is the one before it times a fixed ratio $r$. Its finite sum is $S_N = \dfrac{a\left(1 - r^{N+1}\right)}{1 - r}$, and the infinite series converges to $\dfrac{a}{1 - r}$ exactly when $\lvert r \rvert < 1$; for $\lvert r \rvert \ge 1$ it diverges.
- [Geometry Formulas: Area, Perimeter & Volume for 2D and 3D Shapes](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/geometry): Geometry formulas calculate the perimeter and area of 2D shapes (square, rectangle, triangle, circle) and the surface area and volume of 3D shapes (cube, cuboid, cylinder, cone, sphere). Perimeter uses linear units (cm), area and surface area use square units (cm²), and volume uses cubic units (cm³).
- [Geometry — Concepts, Formulas, Shapes & Topics Guide](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry): Geometry is the branch of mathematics that studies points, lines, angles, shapes, and space — how figures are built, measured, and related. This hub maps every geometry topic into ten clusters: foundations (points, lines, planes), angles, parallel lines and transversals, triangles, quadrilaterals and polygons, circles, 3D solids, coordinate geometry, conic sections, and transformations — each linking to a full guide with formulas and worked examples.
- [Gradient Definition - Meaning & Formula in Maths](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/gradient-definition): The gradient definition in mathematics is the measure of how steeply a line or graph rises or falls: it is the rate of change of $y$ with respect to $x$, calculated as $\dfrac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}$, and it is the same quantity as slope. This guide explains the meaning of gradient as steepness and as a rate, gives six worked examples, and clears up the mistakes that flip or misread the value.
- [Gradient of a Line - Definition & Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/gradient-of-a-line): The gradient of a line measures its steepness and direction: it is the change in $y$ divided by the change in $x$, and it is exactly the $m$ in the equation $y = mx + c$. "Gradient" is the British term for what is called slope elsewhere - they mean the same thing. This guide gives the formula, six examples, and the mistakes that flip or fumble the value.
- [Half Angle Formula — Sin, Cos, Tan, Proof](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/half-angle): The half angle formula expresses $\sin\frac{\theta}{2}$, $\cos\frac{\theta}{2}$, and $\tan\frac{\theta}{2}$ using $\cos\theta$, with a $\pm$ sign fixed by the quadrant the half-angle lands in. This article gives all three identities, the double-angle proof, the quadrant sign rule, six worked examples in degrees and radians (including $15°$, $22.5°$, and $\pi/12$), and the mistakes that cost marks.
- [Harmonic Progression: Formula, Mean & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/harmonic-progression): A Harmonic Progression (HP) is a sequence of non-zero numbers whose reciprocals form an arithmetic progression. So $a_1, a_2, a_3, \dots$ is an HP exactly when $\frac{1}{a_1}, \frac{1}{a_2}, \frac{1}{a_3}, \dots$ is an AP. To find any term, work in the reciprocal AP and flip the result back: $a_n = \dfrac{1}{\frac{1}{a_1} + (n-1)d}$. The harmonic mean of $a$ and $b$ is $\frac{2ab}{a+b}$, and the infinite harmonic series $1 + \frac12 + \frac13 + \cdots$ grows without bound.
- [Height of Equilateral Triangle: Formula, Proof, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/height-of-equilateral-triangle): The height of an equilateral triangle with side $a$ is $h = \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2},a$ — the altitude from any vertex straight down to the opposite side. It comes from the Pythagoras theorem applied to the right triangle the altitude creates. This article gives the formula, its derivation, methods for finding height from the side, area, or perimeter, and six worked examples.
- [Heights and Distances — Trigonometry Formulas & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/heights-and-distances): Heights and distances is the branch of trigonometry that finds the height of an object or the distance to it using a measured angle and one known length — without ever climbing or pacing it out. This article covers the angle of elevation and angle of depression, the line-of-sight idea, the three-step method, the formulas, and six worked examples on towers, poles, and buildings.
- [Hemisphere: Definition, Volume, Surface Area Formulas, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/hemisphere): A hemisphere is a 3D solid formed when a sphere is cut exactly in half through its centre, leaving one flat circular face and one curved face. Its volume is $\frac{2}{3}\pi r^3$, its curved surface area is $2\pi r^2$, and its total surface area is $3\pi r^2$ — the curved part plus the flat circular base. This article derives all three formulas, explains why the total surface area is not simply half a sphere's, and works through the examples and slips students hit most often
- [Hexagon Shape — Definition, Types, Properties, and Area Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/hexagon): Hexagon is a six-sided closed two-dimensional polygon with six vertices and six interior angles. In a regular hexagon, all six sides are equal, all six interior angles are $120°$, and the sum of interior angles is $720°$. The area of a regular hexagon with side $s$ is $\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}s^2$.
- [Hypotenuse - Definition, Formula, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/hypotenuse): The hypotenuse is the side of a right triangle that lies opposite the right angle, and it is always the longest side. You find it with the Pythagorean theorem, $c^2 = a^2 + b^2$; this article covers the definition, the formula, six worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Implicit Differentiation: Steps, Examples & Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/implicit-differentiation): Implicit differentiation finds $\frac{dy}{dx}$ for an equation that is not solved for $y$, such as $x^2 + y^2 = 25$. You differentiate both sides with respect to $x$, treat $y$ as a function of $x$ so every $y$-term picks up a $\frac{dy}{dx}$ through the chain rule, then solve for $\frac{dy}{dx}$. For the circle this gives $\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{x}{y}$, the slope of the tangent at any point.
- [Incenter of a Triangle — Properties, Formula, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/incenter): The incenter of a triangle is the single point where its three angle bisectors meet, and it sits equidistant from all three sides — making it the center of the largest circle that fits inside the triangle. This article covers the definition, the key properties, the coordinate and angle formulas, the inradius, and worked examples, plus how the incenter differs from the circumcenter.
- [Integers Formula — Rules, Properties, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/integers-formula): The integers formula is the set of sign rules that govern arithmetic on $\mathbb{Z} = {\ldots, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, \ldots}$ — same signs add, opposite signs subtract, like signs multiply to positive, unlike signs multiply to negative. This article covers the rules, the closure and associative properties, three worked examples at three difficulty tiers, the Gauss sum-of-first-$n$-integers identity, and the most common sign slips.
- [Integration Formulas: Complete List with Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/integration): Integration formulas are the standard antiderivative results used to solve integrals across Class 11, 12, and JEE - covering basic, trigonometric, inverse trigonometric, exponential, logarithmic, hyperbolic, and special integrals. The general form is ∫f'(x) dx = f(x) + C.
- [Integration by Parts: Formula, LIATE & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/calculus/integration-by-parts): Integration by parts is the technique for integrating a product of two functions, using the formula $\int u,dv = uv - \int v,du$. It comes straight from reversing the product rule of differentiation. The whole skill is choosing which factor to call $u$ (differentiate it) and which to call $dv$ (integrate it), and the LIATE rule tells you how to pick.
- [Integration of UV Formula — ∫u dv = uv − ∫v du](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/integration-of-uv): The integration of uv formula — integration by parts — is $\int u,dv = uv - \int v,du$, the integration counterpart of the product rule for derivatives. This article derives it from the product rule, explains the ILATE rule for choosing $u$, works six examples in increasing difficulty (including the $\int \ln x,dx$ trick), and shows the mistakes that turn a one-line problem into an endless loop.
- [Intercept Form of a Line: Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/intercept-form): The intercept form of a line is $\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1$, where $a$ is the x-intercept (where the line crosses the x-axis) and $b$ is the y-intercept (where it crosses the y-axis). This article covers the formula, its derivation, how to read or graph a line straight from its intercepts, the triangle it cuts with the axes, and six worked examples.
- [Interest Formula – Simple & Compound Interest Explained](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/interest): This article derives and applies both the simple and compound interest formula, walking through every variable, showing worked examples, and explaining when each formula applies in real financial contexts. You will leave able to calculate interest on savings, loans, and investments — and to spot which formula a problem is actually asking for.
- [Interior Angles: Sum Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/interior-angles): Interior angles are the angles inside a polygon, one at each vertex. The sum of all of them is $(n-2) \times 180°$ for an $n$-sided polygon, and in a regular polygon each one equals that sum divided by $n$. This article covers the definition, the sum formula and where it comes from, regular versus irregular polygons, the interior angles between parallel lines, and six worked examples.
- [Internal Division: Formula, Meaning & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/internal-division): Internal division is when a point lies between two endpoints and splits the segment in the ratio $m:n$, with coordinates $\left(\frac{mx_2+nx_1}{m+n}, \frac{my_2+ny_1}{m+n}\right)$. This article explains what internal division means, why the formula works, and how to apply it, with the wider section formula as its parent.
- [Intersection of Two Lines: Formula, Methods, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/intersection-of-two-lines): The intersection of two lines is the single point where they cross, found by solving both line equations together. This guide covers the substitution method, the determinant formula for general-form lines, how to spot parallel lines that never meet, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Inverse Relation - Definition, Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/inverse-relation): An inverse relation is what you get when you swap the two coordinates of every ordered pair in a relation — turning each $(x, y)$ into $(y, x)$. This article defines the inverse relation, shows how its graph reflects across the line $y = x$, proves the inverse relation theorem $(R^{-1})^{-1} = R$, and works through six examples — including why an inverse relation is not always an inverse function.
- [Inverse Trigonometric Functions — Formulas, Domain, Range](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/inverse-trigonometric-functions): The inverse trigonometric functions — $\arcsin$, $\arccos$, $\arctan$, $\arccsc$, $\arcsec$, $\arccot$ — undo the standard trig functions. Each takes a ratio and returns an angle. The trick is that sine, cosine, and tangent each map many angles to the same ratio, so their inverses only work on restricted "principal" intervals: $\arcsin$ on $[-\pi/2, \pi/2]$, $\arccos$ on $[0, \pi]$, $\arctan$ on $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$.
- [Inverse of 2x2 Matrix - Formula, Steps, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/inverse-of-2x2-matrix): The inverse of a $2 \times 2$ matrix $A = \begin{pmatrix} a & b \ c & d \end{pmatrix}$ is given by a clean formula: $A^{-1} = \dfrac{1}{ad - bc}\begin{pmatrix} d & -b \ -c & a \end{pmatrix}$. The denominator $ad - bc$ is the determinant — if it's zero, the inverse doesn't exist.
- [Inverse of Diagonal Matrix — Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/inverse-of-diagonal-matrix): The inverse of a diagonal matrix is found by replacing each diagonal entry with its reciprocal, so $\operatorname{diag}(d_1, d_2, \ldots, d_n)$ inverts to $\operatorname{diag}(1/d_1, 1/d_2, \ldots, 1/d_n)$. This article gives the formula, a short proof, six worked examples, and the one condition that makes the inverse fail.
- [Inverse of a 3x3 Matrix — Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/inverse-of-3x3-matrix): The inverse of a 3x3 matrix $A$ is the matrix $A^{-1}$ with $AA^{-1} = A^{-1}A = I$, found by $A^{-1} = \frac{1}{\det A},\text{adj}(A)$ whenever $\det A \neq 0$. This article covers the 3x3 determinant, the cofactor and adjugate steps, the row-reduction alternative, six worked examples, and the mistakes that quietly break the calculation.
- [Inverse of a Matrix — Definition & Formula](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/inverse-of-a-matrix): The inverse of a matrix $A$ is the square matrix $A^{-1}$ satisfying $AA^{-1} = A^{-1}A = I$, and it exists only when $A$ is square with a non-zero determinant. This article covers what an inverse really is, the fast 2x2 formula, the adjugate-over-determinant method for any size, the invertibility condition, and six worked examples.
- [Involutory Matrix: Definition, Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/involutory-matrix): An involutory matrix is a square matrix that becomes the identity matrix when multiplied by itself, so $A^2 = I$. That single condition means the matrix is its own inverse ($A^{-1} = A$), its determinant is always $+1$ or $-1$, and its eigenvalues can only be $+1$ or $-1$. The identity matrix and every reflection matrix are involutory.
- [Isosceles Right Triangle: Formulas & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/isosceles-right-triangle): An isosceles right triangle is a right triangle whose two legs are equal, giving angles of 45°, 45°, and 90° and a fixed side ratio of 1 : 1 : √2. This article covers its definition, the 45-45-90 angle structure, area ($\tfrac{x^2}{2}$) and hypotenuse ($x\sqrt{2}$) formulas, six worked examples, and the mistakes students make most.
- [Isosceles Triangle - Definition, Types, Formulas](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/isosceles-triangles): An isosceles triangle is a triangle with two sides of equal length — called the legs — and one side of different length called the base. The two angles opposite the equal sides (the base angles) are also equal.
- [Isosceles Triangle Formula — Area, Perimeter, Height](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/isosceles-triangles-formula): The isosceles triangle formula set is: area $= \frac{1}{2}bh$ (or $\frac{b}{2}\sqrt{a^2 - \tfrac{b^2}{4}}$ from the equal side $a$ and base $b$), perimeter $= 2a + b$, and height $= \sqrt{a^2 - \tfrac{b^2}{4}}$. This article gives each formula, derives the height and area straight from the Pythagorean theorem, works six examples from one-step to a word problem, and clears up the mistakes that cost the most marks.
- [Kelvin to Celsius Formula — Steps & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/kelvin-to-celsius-formula): The kelvin to celsius formula is $°C = K - 273.15$ — subtract $273.15$ from the kelvin reading and you have the celsius value. This article gives the one-step formula, worked conversions, a reference table from absolute zero to the Sun's surface, the mistakes that trip students up, and where the conversion actually shows up.
- [Kelvin to Fahrenheit Formula — Steps, Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/kelvin-to-fahrenheit): The kelvin to fahrenheit formula is $°F = (K - 273.15) \times \frac{9}{5} + 32$ — two steps: shift kelvin to celsius by subtracting $273.15$, then scale-and-offset celsius to fahrenheit. This article gives the formula, its derivation from the two scale definitions, three worked examples, a quick-reference conversion table, and the most common mistakes — including the one that helped crash a Mars probe.
- [Lateral Area Formula — All Shapes in One Place](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/lateral-area): The lateral area formula measures the surface area of a solid's side faces only — excluding the top and bottom bases. This article gives the lateral area formula for every standard solid (cube, cuboid, cylinder, cone, pyramid, hemisphere), the one rule that unifies them ($LSA = \text{perimeter} \times \text{height}$ for prisms), three worked examples across difficulty tiers, and the single mistake that catches most students.
- [Lateral Area of a Cube Formula — LSA = 4a²](https://bhanzu.com/math/formula/lateral-area-of-a-cube): The lateral area of a cube formula equals four times the square of the side length: $LSA = 4a^2$. This article gives the formula, the one-line derivation, three worked examples, the side-by-side with total surface area $TSA = 6a^2$, and the most common mistake — including the top-and-bottom faces when "lateral" means side faces only.
- [Latus Rectum of Ellipse: Formula and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/latus-rectum-of-ellipse): The latus rectum of an ellipse is the chord through a focus, perpendicular to the major axis, and its length is $\frac{2b^2}{a}$ for the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ with $a > b$. This article defines it, derives the formula, locates its endpoints, links it to eccentricity, and works six examples.
- [Latus Rectum: Parabola, Ellipse, and Hyperbola Formulas](https://bhanzu.com/math/geometry/latus-rectum): The latus rectum is the focal chord of a conic drawn perpendicular to its main axis, ending on the curve; its length is $4a$ for a parabola $y^2 = 4ax$ and $\dfrac{2b^2}{a}$ for both an ellipse and a hyperbola. This article defines the latus rectum, gives each conic's formula and endpoint coordinates, explains why it measures a conic's width at the focus, and works through examples.
- [Law of Cosines : Formula, Proof, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/law-of-cosine): The law of cosines states $c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab\cos C$, relating the three sides of any triangle to the cosine of one angle. This article covers when to use it (the SAS and SSS cases), its derivation, six worked examples, how it generalises the Pythagorean theorem, and the errors students make with obtuse angles.
- [Law of Sines : Formula, Proof, and Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/trigonometry/sine-law): The law of sines states $\dfrac{a}{\sin A} = \dfrac{b}{\sin B} = \dfrac{c}{\sin C}$, so each side of a triangle is proportional to the sine of its opposite angle. This article covers when to use it (the AAS, ASA, and SSA cases), its proof, the ambiguous case where two triangles fit the data, six worked examples, and the mistakes to avoid.
- [Linear Fractional Transformation: Formula & Examples](https://bhanzu.com/math/algebra/linear-fractional-transformation): A linear fractional transformation is a function of the form $f(x) = \frac{ax+b}{cx+d}$, where $a$, $b$, $c$, $d$ are constants and $ad - bc \neq 0$. It is also called a Möbius transformation, its real graph is a hyperbola, and it can be written as the $2 \times 2$ matrix $\begin{bmatrix} a & b
